English for Work · Health & life sciences

General Practice English

Consultation English for primary-care doctors: histories, uncertain results, treatment expectations, prevention, mental health, and coordinated follow-up.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Negotiate priorities without dismissing a newly disclosed concern.
  • Clarify a patient word and reconstruct a timeline without supplying the answer.
  • Investigate a medication discrepancy while separating actual use from recorded instructions.
  • Explain a result without equating a laboratory flag with a confirmed condition.
  • Explain an assessed recommendation while acknowledging the expectation behind a treatment request.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.

01Agreeing on a consultation agendaThree concerns, one visit+

Your goal

Negotiate priorities without dismissing a newly disclosed concern.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Three concerns, one visit

Dr Shah sees Morgan for a planned long-term-condition review. Morgan also wants to discuss tiredness and a form for work. No clinical urgency assessment has yet been made. Morgan says the tiredness matters most. Dr Shah will first clarify that concern and assess priorities, then explain what can be covered and what requires follow-up. No symptom is declared safe to postpone merely because the appointment was booked for another purpose.

Dr Shah: General practitioner / Morgan: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was originally planned?

Answer and explanation

C. A long-term-condition review

The booking is a review; the other concerns emerge during this consultation.

2. Which concern does Morgan prioritize?

Answer and explanation

B. Tiredness

Morgan explicitly identifies tiredness as the concern that matters most.

3. What has not yet happened?

Answer and explanation

C. Clinical assessment of urgency

The brief says urgency has not been assessed at this point.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A booked review does not determine the urgency of a newly raised symptom. Agree on the agenda after appropriate clinical assessment, not before it.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
consultation agenda
Concerns and tasks agreed for a clinical encounter.agree on the consultation agenda
presenting concern
Problem the patient brings to the current encounter.elicit the presenting concern
continuity of care
Connected care over time with an ongoing clinical relationship.maintain continuity of care
comorbidity
Another condition occurring alongside a particular condition.review relevant comorbidity
multimorbidity
Presence of multiple long-term health conditions in one person.manage multimorbidity
problem list
Organized record of a patient's identified health problems.update the problem list
acute concern
A new or recently changing health concern.assess an acute concern
long-term condition
Health condition requiring ongoing management or follow-up.review a long-term condition
patient-centered care
Care attentive to the person's needs, priorities, and circumstances.provide patient-centered care
functional limitation
Restriction affecting a person's usual activities or abilities.describe a functional limitation
follow-up visit
Later encounter to review progress or unfinished care.arrange a follow-up visit
priority
Issue judged to require attention before other issues.establish the clinical priority
shared agenda
Visit priorities agreed through discussion with the patient.negotiate a shared agenda
visit length
Time allocated or used for an encounter.explain the visit length
care coordination
Organizing related care across people, services, or settings.improve care coordination
concern elicitation
Inviting the patient to describe what needs attention.use open concern elicitation
signposting
Explaining where a conversation is going next.use clear signposting
safety-netting
Explaining what changes require further help and how to obtain it.provide specific safety-netting
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A work form, a symptom, and a chronic-condition review are different tasks. Summarize all three so that prioritizing one does not erase the others.

C Notice the language

An agenda is negotiated, not imposed

Invite the person to name their concerns before announcing the visit structure. A follow-up arrangement is a specific agreement, not a way to quietly drop an unresolved concern.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the agenda
What else were you hoping we could cover?
Reflect the priority
The tiredness is the main concern for you today.
Explain sequencing
I will clarify that concern before we agree on the order.
Avoid dismissal
I am not setting it aside because it was not on the booking.
Keep another task visible
The work form is still on our list.
Negotiate follow-up
Let us agree how to handle anything we cannot complete today.
Use a transition
First I will ask about the tiredness; then we will review the plan.
Check priorities
Have I captured the concerns in the right order?
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
What else
Invites additional concerns without implying that the patient has used up permission to speak.
Before we agree
Makes the order conditional on clarification and assessment.
Still on our list
Reassures the patient that a deferred task has not been forgotten.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response avoids dismissing the symptom?

Answer and explanation

D. Let us clarify the tiredness before deciding the agenda.

Clarification must precede assumptions about priority or the symptom's significance.

2. Which wording preserves the patient's preference?

Answer and explanation

D. Morgan identifies tiredness as the main concern.

The statement records Morgan's priority without inventing a refusal or diagnosis.

3. What does follow-up mean here?

Answer and explanation

D. An agreed later review of unfinished work

A follow-up needs a clear purpose and does not replace necessary assessment.

4. Which signpost is clearest?

Answer and explanation

B. First the tiredness history, then we will agree on the remaining plan.

Naming the history before the planning step makes the conversational sequence clear.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Three concerns, one visit

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Shah: General practitioner / Morgan: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • priority
  • signposting
  • presenting concern
  • consultation agenda
  • shared agenda
  • continuity of care
  • safety-netting
  • follow-up visit
  • functional limitation
  • long-term condition
  1. Dr Shah
    We booked a review of your ongoing condition. Before we start, what else were you hoping to discuss?
  2. Morgan
    I have been unusually tired, and I brought a work form. The tiredness is my main 1.
  3. Dr Shah
    Thank you. I will ask about the tiredness before we decide which issues need attention first.
  4. Morgan
    I thought the 2 was fixed because the reminder only mentioned a review.
  5. Dr Shah
    The booking helps us prepare, but it does not replace listening to a new concern or assessing it.
  6. Morgan
    That helps. I did not want the form to make this look like an entirely administrative 3.
  7. Dr Shah
    Let us keep all three items visible. Tell me first when the tiredness began and how it affects you.
  8. Morgan
    I am struggling with ordinary tasks. I suppose that is a 4.
  9. Dr Shah
    Exactly. Describe those tasks in your own words; I do not need you to use a clinical label.
  10. Morgan
    Can we still review the 5 rather than losing that appointment altogether?
  11. Dr Shah
    Yes, we will account for it in the plan. First I need enough information to assess today's priorities.
  12. Morgan
    I appreciate that 6. I understand why you are asking about the tiredness now.
  13. Dr Shah
    After that assessment, we can agree what to complete today and how to address anything left unfinished.
  14. Morgan
    A specific 7 would help more than being told to call sometime.
  15. Dr Shah
    We will make the purpose clear and explain how to obtain help if the situation changes before then.
  16. Morgan
    So 8 is part of the plan, not a promise that nothing will happen.
  17. Dr Shah
    Correct. We also need to clarify the form's requirements before promising what I can certify.
  18. Morgan
    Then our 9 begins with the tiredness, while the review and form remain visible.
  19. Dr Shah
    That captures your preference. I will assess the concern before we settle the clinical order and next steps.
  20. Morgan
    Good. I want 10, not three disconnected conversations in which the details get lost.

Answers and explanations
  1. presenting concern Turn 2. The presenting concern is the problem Morgan brings for assessment today.
  2. consultation agenda Turn 4. The consultation agenda includes the issues discussed and prioritized for this encounter.
  3. priority Turn 6. Priority identifies what needs attention first rather than what happens to be written on a booking.
  4. functional limitation Turn 8. Functional limitation describes difficulty with usual activities rather than a diagnosis by itself.
  5. long-term condition Turn 10. A long-term condition is an ongoing health issue requiring continuing care and review.
  6. signposting Turn 12. Signposting tells the listener which part of the conversation comes next and why.
  7. follow-up visit Turn 14. A follow-up visit provides an agreed later encounter for review or unfinished care.
  8. safety-netting Turn 16. Safety-netting explains changes that require further help and the route for obtaining it.
  9. shared agenda Turn 18. A shared agenda reflects both the patient's concerns and the clinician's assessed priorities.
  10. continuity of care Turn 20. Continuity of care connects this visit with ongoing review instead of treating every concern as unrelated.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Shah: We booked a review of your ongoing condition. Before we start, what else were you hoping to discuss?
  2. Morgan: I have been unusually tired, and I brought a work form. The tiredness is my main presenting concern.
  3. Dr Shah: Thank you. I will ask about the tiredness before we decide which issues need attention first.
  4. Morgan: I thought the consultation agenda was fixed because the reminder only mentioned a review.
  5. Dr Shah: The booking helps us prepare, but it does not replace listening to a new concern or assessing it.
  6. Morgan: That helps. I did not want the form to make this look like an entirely administrative priority.
  7. Dr Shah: Let us keep all three items visible. Tell me first when the tiredness began and how it affects you.
  8. Morgan: I am struggling with ordinary tasks. I suppose that is a functional limitation.
  9. Dr Shah: Exactly. Describe those tasks in your own words; I do not need you to use a clinical label.
  10. Morgan: Can we still review the long-term condition rather than losing that appointment altogether?
  11. Dr Shah: Yes, we will account for it in the plan. First I need enough information to assess today's priorities.
  12. Morgan: I appreciate that signposting. I understand why you are asking about the tiredness now.
  13. Dr Shah: After that assessment, we can agree what to complete today and how to address anything left unfinished.
  14. Morgan: A specific follow-up visit would help more than being told to call sometime.
  15. Dr Shah: We will make the purpose clear and explain how to obtain help if the situation changes before then.
  16. Morgan: So safety-netting is part of the plan, not a promise that nothing will happen.
  17. Dr Shah: Correct. We also need to clarify the form's requirements before promising what I can certify.
  18. Morgan: Then our shared agenda begins with the tiredness, while the review and form remain visible.
  19. Dr Shah: That captures your preference. I will assess the concern before we settle the clinical order and next steps.
  20. Morgan: Good. I want continuity of care, not three disconnected conversations in which the details get lost.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A second concern before the door closes

A patient adds an issue while the agenda is being agreed.

  1. Patient: I came about my shoulder, but there is another issue I nearly forgot to mention.
  2. GP: Please tell me what it is before we agree on the priorities for today.
  3. Patient: I also need a form signed, although the shoulder is what worries me.
  4. GP: Let us separate the clinical concern from the form and check what each requires.
  5. Patient: Does mentioning the form mean we will have no time for the shoulder?
  6. GP: No. We need to understand the priorities rather than assume the paperwork comes first.
  7. Patient: I would prefer to discuss the pain before deciding how to handle the document.
  8. GP: That preference helps. I will explain what can be addressed and any follow-up needed.
  9. Patient: So we are agreeing on an agenda, not promising that everything is completed today.
  10. GP: Exactly. We will make the next steps clear rather than leave the unfinished item unmentioned.
The referral letter has a different priority

A clinician checks the patient's reason for attending.

  1. GP: The referral mentions tiredness, but I want to hear what matters most to you today.
  2. Patient: The tiredness is important, but I mainly want to understand the recent test message.
  3. GP: Thank you. The referral gives background; it does not replace your account of this visit.
  4. Patient: I worried that asking about the message would seem unrelated to the appointment.
  5. GP: It belongs on the agenda. Let us identify the questions before choosing the sequence.
  6. Patient: I want to know what the result means and who is responsible for the next step.
  7. GP: We can review the actual result and distinguish its interpretation from the follow-up arrangements.
  8. Patient: Please tell me if something requires a separate discussion rather than leaving it vague.
  9. GP: I will. An agreed follow-up should name the issue and the responsible service.
  10. Patient: That makes the visit feel organized without pretending the referral captured every concern.
A routine review with an access barrier

A patient explains why an earlier appointment was missed.

  1. Patient: I missed the previous review because my shift changed, not because I thought it was unnecessary.
  2. GP: Thank you for explaining. I will record the access difficulty rather than assume refusal.
  3. Patient: I can attend some mornings, but I cannot promise availability at the same time every week.
  4. GP: Let us clarify the actual scheduling options and what follow-up is clinically required.
  5. Patient: Does that mean you can guarantee an appointment outside all my work hours?
  6. GP: I cannot guarantee a slot before checking, but we can identify the practical constraint.
  7. Patient: I would appreciate knowing whom to contact if the offered time is impossible.
  8. GP: We will confirm the appropriate contact and the actual arrangements for the next review.
  9. Patient: So a missed appointment does not tell you what I believe about the care.
  10. GP: Correct. The reason needs clarification, and the next plan needs to be workable.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Three concerns, one visit
  1. Read the corrected agenda exchange. Stress tiredness, review, and work form as three separate concerns.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the signposting lines and the distinction between a proposed agenda and assessed priorities.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep a second concern visible

Patient Lee attends a blood-pressure review and raises a new sleep concern. Dr Chen will clarify the sleep concern before agreeing on priorities. A travel form remains unfinished.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Dr Chen: "The planned visit is a blood-pressure ___."

Answer and explanation

A. review

The planned appointment is the review named in the scenario.

2. Lee: "I also want to discuss my ___."

Answer and explanation

A. sleep

Sleep is the new concern Lee raises during this encounter.

3. Dr Chen: "I will first ___ that concern."

Answer and explanation

C. clarify

Clarification comes before deciding the clinical priorities in this scenario.

4. Lee: "The travel form remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unfinished

The form has not been completed or removed from the plan.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: General Practice English
Professional audience: general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 01: Agreeing on a consultation agenda
Original fictional case: Dr Shah sees Morgan for a planned long-term-condition review. Morgan also wants to discuss tiredness and a form for work. No clinical urgency assessment has yet been made. Morgan says the tiredness matters most. Dr Shah will first clarify that concern and assess priorities, then explain what can be covered and what requires follow-up. No symptom is declared safe to postpone merely because the appointment was booked for another purpose.
Language workshop: Three concerns, one visit
Communication goal: Negotiate priorities without dismissing a newly disclosed concern.
Language guidance: A booked review does not determine the urgency of a newly raised symptom. Agree on the agenda after appropriate clinical assessment, not before it.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What else were you hoping we could cover? / The tiredness is the main concern for you today. / I will clarify that concern before we agree on the order. / I am not setting it aside because it was not on the booking. / The work form is still on our list. / Let us agree how to handle anything we cannot complete today. / First I will ask about the tiredness; then we will review the plan. / Have I captured the concerns in the right order? / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: consultation agenda: Concerns and tasks agreed for a clinical encounter. / presenting concern: Problem the patient brings to the current encounter. / continuity of care: Connected care over time with an ongoing clinical relationship. / comorbidity: Another condition occurring alongside a particular condition. / multimorbidity: Presence of multiple long-term health conditions in one person. / problem list: Organized record of a patient's identified health problems. / acute concern: A new or recently changing health concern. / long-term condition: Health condition requiring ongoing management or follow-up. / patient-centered care: Care attentive to the person's needs, priorities, and circumstances. / functional limitation: Restriction affecting a person's usual activities or abilities. / follow-up visit: Later encounter to review progress or unfinished care. / priority: Issue judged to require attention before other issues. / shared agenda: Visit priorities agreed through discussion with the patient. / visit length: Time allocated or used for an encounter. / care coordination: Organizing related care across people, services, or settings. / concern elicitation: Inviting the patient to describe what needs attention. / signposting: Explaining where a conversation is going next. / safety-netting: Explaining what changes require further help and how to obtain it. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected agenda exchange. Stress tiredness, review, and work form as three separate concerns. Swap roles. Repeat the signposting lines and the distinction between a proposed agenda and assessed priorities. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Shah: General practitioner / Morgan: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Patient Lee attends a blood-pressure review and raises a new sleep concern. Dr Chen will clarify the sleep concern before agreeing on priorities. A travel form remains unfinished.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Shah: Correct. We also need to clarify the form's requirements before promising what I can certify.
Morgan: Then our shared agenda begins with the tiredness, while the review and form remain visible.
Dr Shah: That captures your preference. I will assess the concern before we settle the clinical order and next steps.
Morgan: Good. I want continuity of care, not three disconnected conversations in which the details get lost.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Language guidance: A booked review does not determine the urgency of a newly raised symptom. Agree on the agenda after appropriate clinical assessment, not before it.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What else were you hoping we could cover? / The tiredness is the main concern for you today. / I will clarify that concern before we agree on the order. / I am not setting it aside because it was not on the booking. / The work form is still on our list. / Let us agree how to handle anything we cannot complete today. / First I will ask about the tiredness; then we will review the plan. / Have I captured the concerns in the right order? / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: consultation agenda: Concerns and tasks agreed for a clinical encounter. / presenting concern: Problem the patient brings to the current encounter. / continuity of care: Connected care over time with an ongoing clinical relationship. / comorbidity: Another condition occurring alongside a particular condition. / multimorbidity: Presence of multiple long-term health conditions in one person. / problem list: Organized record of a patient's identified health problems. / acute concern: A new or recently changing health concern. / long-term condition: Health condition requiring ongoing management or follow-up. / patient-centered care: Care attentive to the person's needs, priorities, and circumstances. / functional limitation: Restriction affecting a person's usual activities or abilities. / follow-up visit: Later encounter to review progress or unfinished care. / priority: Issue judged to require attention before other issues. / shared agenda: Visit priorities agreed through discussion with the patient. / visit length: Time allocated or used for an encounter. / care coordination: Organizing related care across people, services, or settings. / concern elicitation: Inviting the patient to describe what needs attention. / signposting: Explaining where a conversation is going next. / safety-netting: Explaining what changes require further help and how to obtain it. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected agenda exchange. Stress tiredness, review, and work form as three separate concerns. Swap roles. Repeat the signposting lines and the distinction between a proposed agenda and assessed priorities. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Shah: General practitioner / Morgan: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Patient Lee attends a blood-pressure review and raises a new sleep concern. Dr Chen will clarify the sleep concern before agreeing on priorities. A travel form remains unfinished.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Shah: Correct. We also need to clarify the form's requirements before promising what I can certify.
Morgan: Then our shared agenda begins with the tiredness, while the review and form remain visible.
Dr Shah: That captures your preference. I will assess the concern before we settle the clinical order and next steps.
Morgan: Good. I want continuity of care, not three disconnected conversations in which the details get lost.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Professional audience: general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents

02Taking a precise symptom historyDizziness means different things+

Your goal

Clarify a patient word and reconstruct a timeline without supplying the answer.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Dizziness means different things

Ari uses the word dizzy for episodes that began on Tuesday. Ari describes feeling about to faint, not the room spinning. Episodes are reported after standing, and their duration is not yet clear. Dr Cole has not established a cause. The task is to clarify the account and record relevant unknowns, not diagnose from one trigger or assume unmentioned symptoms are absent.

Dr Cole: General practitioner / Ari: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When did the episodes begin according to Ari?

Answer and explanation

D. Tuesday

Tuesday is the onset Ari reports, not a date independently established by testing.

2. What sensation does Ari describe?

Answer and explanation

B. Feeling about to faint

The brief distinguishes Ari's near-faint feeling from room-spinning language.

3. What remains unclear?

Answer and explanation

B. Episode duration

Duration is specifically unresolved and should not be silently filled in.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A reported trigger is not a demonstrated cause. Dizziness, vertigo, and feeling faint should not be substituted for one another without clarification.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
onset
Time at which a symptom first began.establish the reported onset
duration
Length of time a symptom or episode lasts.clarify episode duration
frequency
How often a symptom or event occurs.describe symptom frequency
trigger
Event or circumstance associated with starting a symptom.identify a reported trigger
relieving factor
Circumstance associated with reducing a symptom.ask about relieving factors
associated symptom
Another symptom occurring alongside the main symptom.ask about associated symptoms
pertinent negative
Relevant absence established through questioning or assessment.document a pertinent negative
progression
Change in a symptom or condition over time.describe symptom progression
episodic
Occurring in separate periods rather than continuously.describe episodic symptoms
persistent
Continuing rather than resolving between distinct episodes.report persistent discomfort
location
Place in the body where a symptom is experienced.specify the symptom location
radiation
Spread of a symptom such as pain to another area.describe pain radiation
character
Quality of a symptom, such as pressure or burning.clarify symptom character
severity
Degree or intensity of a symptom or condition.assess symptom severity
chronology
Order in which events occurred over time.preserve the chronology
review of systems
Structured questioning about symptoms across body systems.conduct a focused review of systems
history of present illness
Organized account of the current clinical problem.summarize the history of present illness
diagnostic uncertainty
Lack of a sufficiently established explanation for the condition.communicate diagnostic uncertainty
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Not asked, not reported, and denied are different. Record an unanswered duration question as unanswered, not as a normal or reassuring duration.

C Notice the language

Let the description precede the label

A familiar word may mean different sensations to different people. Offer neutral clarification and preserve the person's wording before using professional shorthand.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify a label
What does dizzy feel like for you?
Offer a neutral distinction
Is it spinning, feeling faint, or something different?
Locate the onset
When did you first notice it?
Separate two times
Is that the length of one episode or the time since it began?
Avoid a supplied answer
Describe what happens next in your own words.
Keep a gap visible
We have not yet clarified how long each episode lasts.
Attribute the trigger
You report noticing it after standing.
Summarize carefully
I have the sequence; I have not established the cause.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Since versus for
Since introduces a starting point; for introduces a duration. Since Tuesday does not tell us how long one episode lasts.
When versus why
A when question asks about timing; it does not establish why the symptom happens.
Not yet clarified
Names missing information without converting it into an absent symptom.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence distinguishes onset from duration?

Answer and explanation

C. Episodes began Tuesday; the length of each is still unclear.

Tuesday gives the onset; stating that episode length is unclear preserves the unresolved duration.

2. Which phrase overstates the evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. Standing definitely caused the condition.

An association in the history does not by itself demonstrate causation.

3. Which question is least leading?

Answer and explanation

B. What happens during an episode?

The open question invites Ari's account without embedding an unsupported conclusion.

4. What is a pertinent negative?

Answer and explanation

B. A relevant absence established by asking or assessing

An unmentioned symptom cannot be documented as denied without an appropriate question or assessment.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

Dizziness means different things

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Cole: General practitioner / Ari: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • chronology
  • duration
  • onset
  • episodic
  • associated symptom
  • trigger
  • pertinent negative
  • relieving factor
  • frequency
  • diagnostic uncertainty
  1. Dr Cole
    You used the word dizzy on the appointment request. What does that feel like for you?
  2. Ari
    More like I might faint than like the room is spinning. The 1 was Tuesday.
  3. Dr Cole
    Thank you. Did you feel it continuously from Tuesday, or have there been separate episodes?
  4. Ari
    Separate episodes. I suppose 2 describes it, but I cannot time them accurately yet.
  5. Dr Cole
    That is useful to know. How often have you noticed it, and what are you doing when it starts?
  6. Ari
    Standing up seems to be a 3, although I have not kept a record of every episode.
  7. Dr Cole
    I will record that as your observation. What happens next, and what makes the feeling ease?
  8. Ari
    Sitting down seems to help. That is the main 4 I have noticed.
  9. Dr Cole
    When you say it lasts a while, do you mean seconds, minutes, or something you cannot estimate?
  10. Ari
    I cannot estimate the 5. I do not want to give you a number just to complete the question.
  11. Dr Cole
    Please do not guess. I will ask about other symptoms separately rather than treating silence as an answer.
  12. Ari
    So an 6 is something else happening during the same episode?
  13. Dr Cole
    Yes. I also need to understand any changes and how the episodes affect what you can do.
  14. Ari
    The 7 may be increasing, but I should explain what I actually remember.
  15. Dr Cole
    Exactly. Start with Tuesday and describe the later events in order, including anything that is uncertain.
  16. Ari
    That will keep the 8 straight instead of mixing the first episode with the most recent one.
  17. Dr Cole
    I will summarize your account, then assess it. The standing association alone does not establish a diagnosis.
  18. Ari
    I understand the 9. You are listening to the pattern, not promising an explanation yet.
  19. Dr Cole
    Correct. Any relevant absence I record will come from a question or assessment, not from an empty field.
  20. Ari
    Then a 10 is different from something I simply have not mentioned.

Answers and explanations
  1. onset Turn 2. Onset identifies when Ari first noticed the episodes rather than how long each episode lasts.
  2. episodic Turn 4. Episodic means the sensation occurs in separate periods rather than continuously all the time.
  3. trigger Turn 6. A trigger is a circumstance associated with starting the symptom, not proof of its underlying cause.
  4. relieving factor Turn 8. A relieving factor is something the patient reports as reducing the sensation during an episode.
  5. duration Turn 10. Duration is the length of a single episode, which remains unclear in this history.
  6. associated symptom Turn 12. An associated symptom occurs alongside the main concern and needs its own accurate history.
  7. frequency Turn 14. Frequency tells us how often episodes occur, separate from onset, severity, or the length of one episode.
  8. chronology Turn 16. Chronology preserves the order of reported events without rearranging them to fit a presumed explanation.
  9. diagnostic uncertainty Turn 18. Diagnostic uncertainty means the cause is not established, even though the symptom account is becoming clearer.
  10. pertinent negative Turn 20. A pertinent negative is a relevant absence established through questioning or assessment, not through silence.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Cole: You used the word dizzy on the appointment request. What does that feel like for you?
  2. Ari: More like I might faint than like the room is spinning. The onset was Tuesday.
  3. Dr Cole: Thank you. Did you feel it continuously from Tuesday, or have there been separate episodes?
  4. Ari: Separate episodes. I suppose episodic describes it, but I cannot time them accurately yet.
  5. Dr Cole: That is useful to know. How often have you noticed it, and what are you doing when it starts?
  6. Ari: Standing up seems to be a trigger, although I have not kept a record of every episode.
  7. Dr Cole: I will record that as your observation. What happens next, and what makes the feeling ease?
  8. Ari: Sitting down seems to help. That is the main relieving factor I have noticed.
  9. Dr Cole: When you say it lasts a while, do you mean seconds, minutes, or something you cannot estimate?
  10. Ari: I cannot estimate the duration. I do not want to give you a number just to complete the question.
  11. Dr Cole: Please do not guess. I will ask about other symptoms separately rather than treating silence as an answer.
  12. Ari: So an associated symptom is something else happening during the same episode?
  13. Dr Cole: Yes. I also need to understand any changes and how the episodes affect what you can do.
  14. Ari: The frequency may be increasing, but I should explain what I actually remember.
  15. Dr Cole: Exactly. Start with Tuesday and describe the later events in order, including anything that is uncertain.
  16. Ari: That will keep the chronology straight instead of mixing the first episode with the most recent one.
  17. Dr Cole: I will summarize your account, then assess it. The standing association alone does not establish a diagnosis.
  18. Ari: I understand the diagnostic uncertainty. You are listening to the pattern, not promising an explanation yet.
  19. Dr Cole: Correct. Any relevant absence I record will come from a question or assessment, not from an empty field.
  20. Ari: Then a pertinent negative is different from something I simply have not mentioned.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Dizzy does not yet describe the sensation

A clinician clarifies an ambiguous symptom word.

  1. GP: When you say dizzy, what does that feel like in your own words?
  2. Patient: Sometimes it feels as though the room moves, but sometimes I mean that I feel faint.
  3. GP: Those are different descriptions, so let us keep the episodes separate while we clarify them.
  4. Patient: I thought I needed one medical word before I could explain the problem properly.
  5. GP: Your description is useful. We should not force different experiences into one label.
  6. Patient: Do you want to know how long each episode lasts or how often it happens?
  7. GP: Both, but one question at a time: how long is different from how often.
  8. Patient: I can describe a recent episode, although I have not timed every occurrence.
  9. GP: Please distinguish what you remember from anything you measured rather than guessing a precise number.
  10. Patient: Then the history can stay accurate even when I cannot give perfect timing.
An onset date is corrected

A patient corrects the timeline before it enters the record.

  1. Patient: I said the cough began on Monday, but that was when I first called the clinic.
  2. GP: Thank you for correcting it. When do you recall the symptom actually beginning?
  3. Patient: I noticed it over the weekend, although I cannot identify an exact hour.
  4. GP: I will record the approximate onset separately from the date of the call.
  5. Patient: Does changing my answer make the history less trustworthy than leaving the first date?
  6. GP: No. A clear correction improves the account; keeping a known error would be less useful.
  7. Patient: I also want to distinguish the first mild symptom from when it became more noticeable.
  8. GP: That distinction matters. Onset and worsening are different points in the timeline.
  9. Patient: Please read the summary back so I can check that those points remain separate.
  10. GP: I will, and we will preserve the uncertainty instead of inventing an exact time.
A symptom changes with context

A clinician asks about circumstances without assuming a cause.

  1. GP: You mentioned that the discomfort is more noticeable at work. What changes in that setting?
  2. Patient: I stand longer there, but I do not know whether standing is actually the cause.
  3. GP: That is a useful distinction: association with a setting does not prove the explanation.
  4. Patient: I was concerned that you might write occupational injury before assessing what happened.
  5. GP: I will document your account and investigate the relevant context without assigning an unsupported cause.
  6. Patient: At home I sometimes notice it too, so work is not the only setting.
  7. GP: Let us include that detail rather than describe the symptom as occurring exclusively at work.
  8. Patient: Would a description of the activity be more useful than guessing which diagnosis fits?
  9. GP: Yes. Explain what you were doing, what you felt, and how the episode changed.
  10. Patient: Then we can keep the history specific without treating my theory as a confirmed finding.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Dizziness means different things
  1. Read Ari's corrected symptom history. Contrast onset, duration, and frequency with clear stress.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the neutral clarification question and the summary that preserves diagnostic uncertainty.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate the first day from episode length

Jamie reports intermittent tingling beginning Friday. Individual episodes last about two minutes by Jamie's estimate. Dr Park has not established a cause.

Answer and explanation

A. Friday

Friday identifies when Jamie says the intermittent tingling first began.

2. Jamie: "One episode lasts about ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

C. two

Two is Jamie's estimate of a single episode's duration.

3. Dr Park: "The symptoms are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. intermittent

Intermittent describes episodes that occur with intervals between them.

4. Dr Park: "The cause is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. established

The scenario supplies a symptom account but no confirmed explanation.

Vocabulary: onset: Time at which a symptom first began. / duration: Length of time a symptom or episode lasts. / frequency: How often a symptom or event occurs. / trigger: Event or circumstance associated with starting a symptom. / relieving factor: Circumstance associated with reducing a symptom. / associated symptom: Another symptom occurring alongside the main symptom. / pertinent negative: Relevant absence established through questioning or assessment. / progression: Change in a symptom or condition over time. / episodic: Occurring in separate periods rather than continuously. / persistent: Continuing rather than resolving between distinct episodes. / location: Place in the body where a symptom is experienced. / radiation: Spread of a symptom such as pain to another area. / character: Quality of a symptom, such as pressure or burning. / severity: Degree or intensity of a symptom or condition. / chronology: Order in which events occurred over time. / review of systems: Structured questioning about symptoms across body systems. / history of present illness: Organized account of the current clinical problem. / diagnostic uncertainty: Lack of a sufficiently established explanation for the condition. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play

Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.

03Reconciling medicines without blameTwo lists and an unfamiliar packet+

Your goal

Investigate a medication discrepancy while separating actual use from recorded instructions.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Two lists and an unfamiliar packet

Robin brings a printed hospital list and a packet bought without a prescription. The clinic list differs from the hospital list. Dr Lin has not verified which instructions are current or whether products share an ingredient. Robin sometimes misses a medicine because of cost. No dose changes are supplied. The conversation must establish actual use, verify the conflicting records, and address the cost barrier without calling Robin noncompliant.

Dr Lin: General practitioner / Robin: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which records differ?

Answer and explanation

A. The clinic and hospital lists

The case explicitly identifies a discrepancy between the two medicine lists.

2. What barrier does Robin report?

Answer and explanation

C. Cost

Robin reports missed medicine because of cost, not a verified clinical instruction.

3. What has not been verified?

Answer and explanation

C. Whether the products share an ingredient

The case does not establish the contents or overlap of the products.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Do not treat a different brand name as proof of a different ingredient. Verify products and instructions before resolving the clinical question.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
medication reconciliation
Comparing medicine information to resolve discrepancies across records and actual use.perform medication reconciliation
medicine list
Record of medicines and their relevant instructions.verify the medicine list
over-the-counter product
Product available without a prescription under local rules.disclose over-the-counter products
active ingredient
Substance responsible for a medicine's intended pharmacological effect.compare active ingredients
brand name
Commercial name used to market a product.record the brand name
generic name
Standard nonproprietary name of a medicinal substance.confirm the generic name
duplicate therapy
Unintended or unnecessary overlap between therapeutic products or treatments.check for duplicate therapy
adherence
Extent to which actual medicine use matches an agreed plan.discuss barriers to adherence
adverse reaction
Unwanted harmful response associated with a medicinal product.describe an adverse reaction
drug allergy
Immune-mediated reaction to a medicine.clarify a reported drug allergy
intolerance
Difficulty tolerating a medicine that is not necessarily an allergy.distinguish intolerance from allergy
interaction
Effect of one substance or treatment on another.assess a possible interaction
dosage form
Physical preparation in which a medicine is supplied.verify the dosage form
route of administration
Path by which a medicine enters the body.confirm the route of administration
discontinued medicine
Medicine recorded as no longer intended for use.verify a discontinued medicine
record discrepancy
Difference between records requiring investigation or clarification.resolve a record discrepancy
prescriber
Authorized professional issuing a medicine prescription.contact the original prescriber
medication review
Structured evaluation of medicine use and related needs.arrange a medication review
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

An adverse reaction, an intolerance, and an allergy are not interchangeable labels. Preserve the reported reaction and obtain the relevant clinical history.

C Notice the language

Ask what happens, not whether someone obeys

A list records an instruction or history; it does not prove what the person takes. Ask about practical barriers in neutral language.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about actual use
Walk me through what you actually take.
Normalize disclosure
Please include products bought without a prescription.
Name the discrepancy
These two lists do not match.
Avoid premature correction
I need to verify the current instructions first.
Ask about affordability
Has cost made the agreed plan difficult to follow?
Clarify a reaction
What happened when you took it?
Separate names
Different brand names can still contain the same ingredient.
Agree on verification
We will check the source before finalizing the list.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Actually take
Distinguishes real use from instructions recorded elsewhere.
What happened
Elicits the reaction instead of confirming an assumed allergy.
Before finalizing
Identifies a verification step that must precede a settled record.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question avoids blame?

Answer and explanation

D. Has cost made the plan difficult to follow?

Asking about cost explores a practical barrier without assuming motives or misconduct.

2. Which conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. Different brands prove different ingredients.

Brand names alone do not establish the active ingredients or possible overlap.

3. Which distinction matters?

Answer and explanation

B. Actual use versus recorded instructions

Recorded instructions do not establish what a patient actually takes at home.

4. What should happen before finalizing the list?

Answer and explanation

A. Verification of conflicting information

Verification is needed because neither conflicting record has been established as current.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Two lists and an unfamiliar packet

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Lin: General practitioner / Robin: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • adherence
  • medication reconciliation
  • medication review
  • drug allergy
  • over-the-counter product
  • brand name
  • intolerance
  • active ingredient
  • medicine list
  • record discrepancy
  1. Dr Lin
    Please show me both lists, and include anything you buy yourself or take only occasionally.
  2. Robin
    I brought this 1. I did not think it belonged with my prescriptions.
  3. Dr Lin
    It matters too. First, tell me what you actually take rather than reading only the printed instructions.
  4. Robin
    My hospital 2 differs from the clinic version, so I am uncertain which is current.
  5. Dr Lin
    We need to verify that difference. I will not silently choose one record because it looks newer.
  6. Robin
    Is this what you mean by 3 rather than simply adding another packet to the computer?
  7. Dr Lin
    Yes. We will check the products, instructions, and sources, and establish which questions remain unresolved.
  8. Robin
    This packet has a different 4. Does that mean it cannot overlap with another medicine?
  9. Dr Lin
    Not necessarily. We need the ingredient information before drawing that conclusion or making a clinical decision.
  10. Robin
    Then we should compare the 5, not rely on the appearance of the packaging.
  11. Dr Lin
    Correct. Also, how manageable has the agreed medicine plan been in everyday life?
  12. Robin
    Cost has affected my 6. Sometimes I cannot afford to collect a medicine.
  13. Dr Lin
    Thank you for telling me. That is important information for planning care, not a reason to blame you.
  14. Robin
    I also described one medicine as an allergy, but I am unsure whether it was 7.
  15. Dr Lin
    Tell me exactly what happened and when. We should preserve the reaction details before settling the label.
  16. Robin
    I understand. A recorded 8 should not be invented from an incomplete description.
  17. Dr Lin
    I will clarify the clinical history and contact the relevant source about the conflicting instructions.
  18. Robin
    Please make the 9 visible until it is resolved, so another person does not assume it was settled.
  19. Dr Lin
    Agreed. We have not decided any dose change in this discussion; that requires the appropriate clinical review.
  20. Robin
    Then today's 10 will include affordability as well as an accurate list.

Answers and explanations
  1. over-the-counter product Turn 2. An over-the-counter product is bought without a prescription and still belongs in a medicine review.
  2. medicine list Turn 4. A medicine list records products and instructions but does not prove current actual use.
  3. medication reconciliation Turn 6. Medication reconciliation compares records with actual use and resolves discrepancies rather than merely copying a list.
  4. brand name Turn 8. A brand name is a commercial product name and does not by itself identify a different medicinal ingredient.
  5. active ingredient Turn 10. The active ingredient is the medicinal substance being compared to investigate possible product overlap.
  6. adherence Turn 12. Adherence describes actual use in relation to the agreed plan and can be affected by practical barriers.
  7. intolerance Turn 14. Intolerance means difficulty tolerating a medicine and is not automatically an immune-mediated allergy.
  8. drug allergy Turn 16. A drug allergy refers to an immune-mediated reaction and needs accurate history rather than an assumed label.
  9. record discrepancy Turn 18. A record discrepancy is an unresolved difference between medicine records that needs verification.
  10. medication review Turn 20. The medication review evaluates actual use, product information, barriers, and unresolved clinical questions together.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Lin: Please show me both lists, and include anything you buy yourself or take only occasionally.
  2. Robin: I brought this over-the-counter product. I did not think it belonged with my prescriptions.
  3. Dr Lin: It matters too. First, tell me what you actually take rather than reading only the printed instructions.
  4. Robin: My hospital medicine list differs from the clinic version, so I am uncertain which is current.
  5. Dr Lin: We need to verify that difference. I will not silently choose one record because it looks newer.
  6. Robin: Is this what you mean by medication reconciliation rather than simply adding another packet to the computer?
  7. Dr Lin: Yes. We will check the products, instructions, and sources, and establish which questions remain unresolved.
  8. Robin: This packet has a different brand name. Does that mean it cannot overlap with another medicine?
  9. Dr Lin: Not necessarily. We need the ingredient information before drawing that conclusion or making a clinical decision.
  10. Robin: Then we should compare the active ingredient, not rely on the appearance of the packaging.
  11. Dr Lin: Correct. Also, how manageable has the agreed medicine plan been in everyday life?
  12. Robin: Cost has affected my adherence. Sometimes I cannot afford to collect a medicine.
  13. Dr Lin: Thank you for telling me. That is important information for planning care, not a reason to blame you.
  14. Robin: I also described one medicine as an allergy, but I am unsure whether it was intolerance.
  15. Dr Lin: Tell me exactly what happened and when. We should preserve the reaction details before settling the label.
  16. Robin: I understand. A recorded drug allergy should not be invented from an incomplete description.
  17. Dr Lin: I will clarify the clinical history and contact the relevant source about the conflicting instructions.
  18. Robin: Please make the record discrepancy visible until it is resolved, so another person does not assume it was settled.
  19. Dr Lin: Agreed. We have not decided any dose change in this discussion; that requires the appropriate clinical review.
  20. Robin: Then today's medication review will include affordability as well as an accurate list.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An allergy label needs clarification

The medicine record contains an imprecise reaction entry.

  1. Patient: My record says allergy, but I remember nausea rather than a rash or breathing problem.
  2. GP: Let us clarify what happened and when, without changing the label casually.
  3. Patient: Does that mean you are saying it definitely was not an allergy?
  4. GP: No. Your description needs appropriate clinical review before the record is amended.
  5. Patient: I can explain the symptoms, but I do not remember the exact product name.
  6. GP: We should preserve that uncertainty and check the available records rather than guess.
  7. Patient: I also took another medicine at the time, which might matter to the review.
  8. GP: Please include that context, while keeping an association separate from a proven cause.
  9. Patient: So we will document the actual reaction history and ask the responsible team to review it.
  10. GP: Exactly. A precise account is more useful than either retaining or removing a label without assessment.
A supplement is missing from the list

A patient asks whether a nonprescription product belongs in reconciliation.

  1. GP: Is there anything you take that does not appear on this list, including supplements?
  2. Patient: I use a herbal product, but I did not think it counted as medicine.
  3. GP: It belongs in the review. Please bring the actual product information rather than a guessed name.
  4. Patient: I assumed natural meant it could not affect anything prescribed by the clinic.
  5. GP: That assumption is not reliable; the actual ingredients and clinical context need checking.
  6. Patient: I cannot remember the amount, and the packaging is at home.
  7. GP: Then we will record that it remains unverified rather than invent a dose.
  8. Patient: Should the list distinguish what I currently take from something I stopped months ago?
  9. GP: Yes. Current use, past use, and an unverified product are different statuses.
  10. Patient: I can see why a complete list means more than copying the prescription screen.
Cost changed what was actually taken

A clinician clarifies a gap between prescription and use.

  1. Patient: The prescription list says I take it regularly, but cost has made that difficult.
  2. GP: Thank you for saying so. A prescription record is not proof of actual use.
  3. Patient: I was afraid the note would simply say noncompliant without explaining the problem.
  4. GP: We should describe the access barrier and what happened, not substitute a judgment.
  5. Patient: I do not want to change the medicine on my own to make it last longer.
  6. GP: Any change needs the responsible clinical review, not an improvised adjustment.
  7. Patient: Could someone help check the actual assistance or coverage options available to me?
  8. GP: We can identify the appropriate support route without promising approval before it is verified.
  9. Patient: Please also make sure the record distinguishes the intended plan from what I could obtain.
  10. GP: That distinction belongs in reconciliation and in the discussion of the next safe clinical plan.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two lists and an unfamiliar packet
  1. Read the corrected review. Stress actually take, recorded instructions, and verify as distinct ideas.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the affordability question and the reaction-history question without adding blame or a dose change.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Verify an old entry

Taylor says an outside prescriber stopped one medicine, but the local list still includes it. Dr Noor will verify the instruction. The date is unknown; no new dose has been authorized.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Taylor: "An outside ___ gave the instruction."

Answer and explanation

A. prescriber

The reported instruction comes from the outside prescriber in this case.

2. Dr Noor: "The local list still ___ the medicine."

Answer and explanation

B. includes

The unresolved local entry is explicitly described as still including the medicine.

3. Taylor: "The instruction date is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unknown

The date has not been supplied and must not be guessed.

4. Dr Noor: "I will ___ the instruction."

Answer and explanation

B. verify

Verification is required before treating the reported instruction as settled.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
