Lesson 03: Reconciling medicines without blame
Original fictional case: Robin brings a printed hospital list and a packet bought without a prescription. The clinic list differs from the hospital list. Dr Lin has not verified which instructions are current or whether products share an ingredient. Robin sometimes misses a medicine because of cost. No dose changes are supplied. The conversation must establish actual use, verify the conflicting records, and address the cost barrier without calling Robin noncompliant.
Language workshop: Two lists and an unfamiliar packet
Communication goal: Investigate a medication discrepancy while separating actual use from recorded instructions.
Language guidance: Do not treat a different brand name as proof of a different ingredient. Verify products and instructions before resolving the clinical question.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Walk me through what you actually take. / Please include products bought without a prescription. / These two lists do not match. / I need to verify the current instructions first. / Has cost made the agreed plan difficult to follow? / What happened when you took it? / Different brand names can still contain the same ingredient. / We will check the source before finalizing the list. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: medication reconciliation: Comparing medicine information to resolve discrepancies across records and actual use. / medicine list: Record of medicines and their relevant instructions. / over-the-counter product: Product available without a prescription under local rules. / active ingredient: Substance responsible for a medicine's intended pharmacological effect. / brand name: Commercial name used to market a product. / generic name: Standard nonproprietary name of a medicinal substance. / duplicate therapy: Unintended or unnecessary overlap between therapeutic products or treatments. / adherence: Extent to which actual medicine use matches an agreed plan. / adverse reaction: Unwanted harmful response associated with a medicinal product. / drug allergy: Immune-mediated reaction to a medicine. / intolerance: Difficulty tolerating a medicine that is not necessarily an allergy. / interaction: Effect of one substance or treatment on another. / dosage form: Physical preparation in which a medicine is supplied. / route of administration: Path by which a medicine enters the body. / discontinued medicine: Medicine recorded as no longer intended for use. / record discrepancy: Difference between records requiring investigation or clarification. / prescriber: Authorized professional issuing a medicine prescription. / medication review: Structured evaluation of medicine use and related needs. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected review. Stress actually take, recorded instructions, and verify as distinct ideas. Swap roles. Repeat the affordability question and the reaction-history question without adding blame or a dose change. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.

Robin: Please make the record discrepancy visible until it is resolved, so another person does not assume it was settled.
Dr Lin: Agreed. We have not decided any dose change in this discussion; that requires the appropriate clinical review.
Robin: Then today's medication review will include affordability as well as an accurate list.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Explaining an uncertain test resultOutside the range is not the diagnosis+

Your goal

Explain a result without equating a laboratory flag with a confirmed condition.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Outside the range is not the diagnosis

Casey sees an out-of-range flag on a blood-test report before speaking with Dr Vega. No numerical value or diagnosis is supplied in this case. Dr Vega will review the result alongside symptoms, previous results, and sample context. A repeat test is being considered, not yet ordered. Casey fears that the flag means a serious diagnosis is certain.

Dr Vega: General practitioner / Casey: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has Casey seen?

Answer and explanation

C. An out-of-range flag

The brief supplies a laboratory flag but no confirmed diagnosis or numerical value.

2. What is the repeat-test status?

Answer and explanation

D. Under consideration

A repeat is being considered and has not yet been ordered.

3. What will Dr Vega use alongside the result?

Answer and explanation

D. Symptoms, previous results, and sample context

Interpretation requires the stated clinical and sample context, not the flag alone.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Outside a reference interval is not a diagnosis. Normal, negative, and no further action are also different statements, each requiring context.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
laboratory result
Reported outcome of a test on a specimen.interpret a laboratory result
reference interval
Range used by a laboratory for comparison with a result.explain the reference interval
out-of-range flag
Indicator that a result lies outside a stated comparison range.review an out-of-range flag
trend
Pattern of change across comparable observations over time.examine the result trend
baseline
Earlier or usual state used for comparison.compare with the baseline
preanalytical variation
Differences arising before a sample is analyzed.review preanalytical variation
false-negative result
Negative test result when the target condition is actually present.explain a false-negative result
false-positive result
Positive test result when the target condition is actually absent.explain a false-positive result
repeat sample
New specimen obtained to repeat an investigation.request a repeat sample
incidental finding
Finding discovered outside the main purpose of an investigation.explain an incidental finding
clinical significance
Meaning or importance of a finding for patient care.assess clinical significance
differential diagnosis
Set of possible explanations being evaluated clinically.discuss the differential diagnosis
working diagnosis
Provisional explanation guiding assessment or care.qualify a working diagnosis
confirmatory test
Investigation used to help establish a suspected finding.discuss a confirmatory test
sensitivity
Ability of a test to identify people with the target condition.explain test sensitivity
specificity
Ability of a test to identify people without the target condition.explain test specificity
predictive value
How strongly a result predicts the target condition in its context.discuss predictive value
pending result
Test outcome that has not yet become available.track a pending result
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A possible repeat is not an order, and an order is not a completed result. Keep the stage of the investigation explicit.

C Notice the language

Explain the flag before offering reassurance

A patient may encounter technical results before receiving an explanation. Acknowledge the uncertainty and explain the review process without promising that a flagged result is harmless.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the worry
Seeing a flag before an explanation can be unsettling.
Explain the marker
The flag shows how the result compares with the laboratory range.
Limit the conclusion
The flag alone does not establish a diagnosis.
Bring in context
I will compare it with your symptoms and previous results.
State a proposal
A repeat is one option under consideration.
Avoid false reassurance
I cannot call it harmless before reviewing the context.
Clarify status
No repeat has been ordered yet.
Check the meaning
What does the flag mean to you after that explanation?
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Does not establish
Limits a conclusion without claiming that the opposite has been proved.
Under consideration
Describes a proposal rather than a completed clinical action.
Compared with
Introduces the reference used to interpret a result or trend.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. The flag needs clinical context.

A laboratory comparison marker cannot replace interpretation in the person's clinical context.

2. Which statement preserves action status?

Answer and explanation

A. A repeat is being considered but has not been ordered.

The proposed test has not advanced to an order or completed result.

3. Which reassurance is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

C. There is definitely nothing to worry about.

The case supplies no basis for guaranteeing that the result has no clinical importance.

4. What is a working diagnosis?

Answer and explanation

C. A provisional explanation

A working diagnosis remains provisional and may change as evidence develops.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Outside the range is not the diagnosis

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Vega: General practitioner / Casey: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • repeat sample
  • clinical significance
  • out-of-range flag
  • baseline
  • trend
  • reference interval
  • working diagnosis
  • confirmatory test
  • preanalytical variation
  • pending result
  1. Casey
    The portal highlighted my blood result. I saw the warning before anyone explained what it meant.
  2. Dr Vega
    I understand why the 1 worried you. Let us separate the marker from its clinical meaning.
  3. Casey
    Does outside the range mean that I definitely have the condition I read about online?
  4. Dr Vega
    Not by itself. The 2 helps comparison, but we must interpret the result in context.
  5. Casey
    What context do you need before you can tell me more about this particular result?
  6. Dr Vega
    Your symptoms, earlier results, and the sample circumstances help us assess its 3.
  7. Casey
    I remember having the same test previously. Will you compare those reports rather than only this one?
  8. Dr Vega
    Yes, where they are comparable. A 4 can add information that one isolated result cannot provide.
  9. Casey
    I do not know whether the earlier value was typical for me or just another unusual day.
  10. Dr Vega
    We should not assume it is your 5 without reviewing the relevant history and circumstances.
  11. Casey
    Could something about the sample affect the result, or would that just be an excuse to ignore it?
  12. Dr Vega
    Sample context can matter. Reviewing 6 does not mean declaring that the laboratory made an error.
  13. Casey
    Are you ordering the test again now, or is that only something we might discuss?
  14. Dr Vega
    A 7 is under consideration. I have not ordered it in this discussion.
  15. Casey
    So there is no new result waiting somewhere that I have failed to look at?
  16. Dr Vega
    Correct. A 8 would be a different situation from considering whether to repeat the test.
  17. Casey
    I would rather hear that something is uncertain than be reassured today and surprised tomorrow.
  18. Dr Vega
    That is reasonable. A 9 must be described as provisional while we evaluate the evidence.
  19. Casey
    I understand the difference now: the flag needs review, but it is not the diagnosis itself.
  20. Dr Vega
    Exactly. We will explain the plan, including whether a 10 is appropriate and who will communicate any subsequent result.

Answers and explanations
  1. out-of-range flag Turn 2. An out-of-range flag compares a result with a stated interval without establishing a diagnosis.
  2. reference interval Turn 4. A reference interval is a laboratory comparison range, not a stand-alone diagnosis for an individual patient.
  3. clinical significance Turn 6. Clinical significance concerns what the finding means for care, beyond whether the laboratory flagged it.
  4. trend Turn 8. A trend is the pattern across comparable results over time rather than one isolated flagged value.
  5. baseline Turn 10. A baseline is an earlier or usual state used for comparison and must be established appropriately.
  6. preanalytical variation Turn 12. Preanalytical variation concerns differences arising before analysis, which may be relevant without proving an error occurred.
  7. repeat sample Turn 14. A repeat sample would be a new specimen for another test; here it remains a possibility rather than an order.
  8. pending result Turn 16. A pending result belongs to an investigation whose outcome is not yet available, not to a test merely being considered.
  9. working diagnosis Turn 18. A working diagnosis is a provisional explanation and should be distinguished from a confirmed conclusion.
  10. confirmatory test Turn 20. A confirmatory test helps establish a suspected finding when clinically appropriate; none is automatically required by a flag alone.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Casey: The portal highlighted my blood result. I saw the warning before anyone explained what it meant.
  2. Dr Vega: I understand why the out-of-range flag worried you. Let us separate the marker from its clinical meaning.
  3. Casey: Does outside the range mean that I definitely have the condition I read about online?
  4. Dr Vega: Not by itself. The reference interval helps comparison, but we must interpret the result in context.
  5. Casey: What context do you need before you can tell me more about this particular result?
  6. Dr Vega: Your symptoms, earlier results, and the sample circumstances help us assess its clinical significance.
  7. Casey: I remember having the same test previously. Will you compare those reports rather than only this one?
  8. Dr Vega: Yes, where they are comparable. A trend can add information that one isolated result cannot provide.
  9. Casey: I do not know whether the earlier value was typical for me or just another unusual day.
  10. Dr Vega: We should not assume it is your baseline without reviewing the relevant history and circumstances.
  11. Casey: Could something about the sample affect the result, or would that just be an excuse to ignore it?
  12. Dr Vega: Sample context can matter. Reviewing preanalytical variation does not mean declaring that the laboratory made an error.
  13. Casey: Are you ordering the test again now, or is that only something we might discuss?
  14. Dr Vega: A repeat sample is under consideration. I have not ordered it in this discussion.
  15. Casey: So there is no new result waiting somewhere that I have failed to look at?
  16. Dr Vega: Correct. A pending result would be a different situation from considering whether to repeat the test.
  17. Casey: I would rather hear that something is uncertain than be reassured today and surprised tomorrow.
  18. Dr Vega: That is reasonable. A working diagnosis must be described as provisional while we evaluate the evidence.
  19. Casey: I understand the difference now: the flag needs review, but it is not the diagnosis itself.
  20. Dr Vega: Exactly. We will explain the plan, including whether a confirmatory test is appropriate and who will communicate any subsequent result.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The portal flag is not the whole result

A patient asks about a result marked outside range.

  1. Patient: The portal highlighted one result in red, and I assumed that meant a serious diagnosis.
  2. GP: The flag indicates a comparison with a reference range; interpretation needs the actual context.
  3. Patient: Does being outside the range always mean the same thing for every test?
  4. GP: No. The test, degree of difference, history, and other findings can matter.
  5. Patient: I would like you to explain this result rather than reassure me from the color alone.
  6. GP: That is appropriate. We should discuss what the result can and cannot establish.
  7. Patient: I also noticed an earlier value, but I do not know whether the tests are comparable.
  8. GP: We need to check that before describing a meaningful change.
  9. Patient: So the display helps me notice the result, but it does not explain the diagnosis.
  10. GP: Exactly. We will use the actual report and clinical assessment rather than the portal's color.
A repeat request needs a purpose

A patient asks why another sample is proposed.

  1. GP: The team has requested another sample, and I want to explain the actual reason clearly.
  2. Patient: I thought a repeat meant the first result must have been wrong.
  3. GP: Not necessarily. We need to distinguish checking a finding from declaring the first result invalid.
  4. Patient: Could you tell me what question the repeat is intended to answer?
  5. GP: Yes. The explanation should connect the actual request to the clinical question.
  6. Patient: I do not want to assume that repeating the test guarantees a normal result.
  7. GP: It cannot guarantee that. The possible findings and next discussion still require review.
  8. Patient: Who will explain the result once the new sample has been processed?
  9. GP: We should identify the responsible clinician and the actual contact arrangements.
  10. Patient: That gives me a purpose and a follow-up route without predicting the result.
A comparison uses a different laboratory

A clinician checks whether two reported values can be compared directly.

  1. Patient: These two results look different, but they came from different laboratories several months apart.
  2. GP: We should check the units, methods, reference information, and clinical context before interpreting the difference.
  3. Patient: I nearly subtracted the numbers and described the answer as a definite deterioration.
  4. GP: That arithmetic alone would not establish what the change means clinically.
  5. Patient: One report also uses an abbreviation that is absent from the other.
  6. GP: Let us clarify the actual test names rather than assume the labels are interchangeable.
  7. Patient: Would the dates and any relevant circumstances around sampling belong in the discussion?
  8. GP: Yes. A comparison needs more than two isolated numbers placed beside each other.
  9. Patient: So we can acknowledge the apparent difference without declaring a diagnosis from it.
  10. GP: Correct. We will explain the reviewed comparison and preserve any important limitation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Outside the range is not the diagnosis
  1. Read the corrected results discussion. Contrast flag, interpretation, and diagnosis.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the lines distinguishing a possible repeat, an order, and a pending result.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A result still awaiting review

Sam sees a report in the portal. Dr Ames has received it but has not completed the clinical review. The report is available; its clinical meaning and next action remain to be discussed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Sam: "The report is now ___."

Answer and explanation

D. available

The portal report exists and can be seen in this scenario.

2. Dr Ames: "The clinical review is not ___."

Answer and explanation

D. completed

Receiving a report does not mean its clinical review has been completed.

3. Sam: "A visible result is not a confirmed ___."

Answer and explanation

B. diagnosis

The scenario provides a report but does not establish a diagnosis.

4. Dr Ames: "We still need to discuss the next ___."

Answer and explanation

A. action

The next clinical action remains unresolved in the supplied facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Explaining an uncertain test result
Original fictional case: Casey sees an out-of-range flag on a blood-test report before speaking with Dr Vega. No numerical value or diagnosis is supplied in this case. Dr Vega will review the result alongside symptoms, previous results, and sample context. A repeat test is being considered, not yet ordered. Casey fears that the flag means a serious diagnosis is certain.
Language workshop: Outside the range is not the diagnosis
Communication goal: Explain a result without equating a laboratory flag with a confirmed condition.
Language guidance: Outside a reference interval is not a diagnosis. Normal, negative, and no further action are also different statements, each requiring context.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Seeing a flag before an explanation can be unsettling. / The flag shows how the result compares with the laboratory range. / The flag alone does not establish a diagnosis. / I will compare it with your symptoms and previous results. / A repeat is one option under consideration. / I cannot call it harmless before reviewing the context. / No repeat has been ordered yet. / What does the flag mean to you after that explanation? / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: laboratory result: Reported outcome of a test on a specimen. / reference interval: Range used by a laboratory for comparison with a result. / out-of-range flag: Indicator that a result lies outside a stated comparison range. / trend: Pattern of change across comparable observations over time. / baseline: Earlier or usual state used for comparison. / preanalytical variation: Differences arising before a sample is analyzed. / false-negative result: Negative test result when the target condition is actually present. / false-positive result: Positive test result when the target condition is actually absent. / repeat sample: New specimen obtained to repeat an investigation. / incidental finding: Finding discovered outside the main purpose of an investigation. / clinical significance: Meaning or importance of a finding for patient care. / differential diagnosis: Set of possible explanations being evaluated clinically. / working diagnosis: Provisional explanation guiding assessment or care. / confirmatory test: Investigation used to help establish a suspected finding. / sensitivity: Ability of a test to identify people with the target condition. / specificity: Ability of a test to identify people without the target condition. / predictive value: How strongly a result predicts the target condition in its context. / pending result: Test outcome that has not yet become available. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected results discussion. Contrast flag, interpretation, and diagnosis. Swap roles. Repeat the lines distinguishing a possible repeat, an order, and a pending result. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Vega: General practitioner / Casey: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Sam sees a report in the portal. Dr Ames has received it but has not completed the clinical review. The report is available; its clinical meaning and next action remain to be discussed.

Casey: I understand the difference now: the flag needs review, but it is not the diagnosis itself.
Dr Vega: Exactly. We will explain the plan, including whether a confirmatory test is appropriate and who will communicate any subsequent result.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Responding to an antibiotic requestA recommendation without a dismissal+

Your goal

Explain an assessed recommendation while acknowledging the expectation behind a treatment request.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A recommendation without a dismissal

After an assessment, Dr Rao has not identified an indication for an antibiotic in this fictional visit. Jules expected one because a previous illness improved after antibiotics. The case does not provide diagnostic findings, a named infection, or medicine instructions. The conversation explains the current recommendation, distinguishes sequence from proof of benefit, and includes an individualized follow-up and safety-net discussion with the actual clinician.

Dr Rao: General practitioner / Jules: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the supplied recommendation based on?

Answer and explanation

D. Dr Rao's assessment in this visit

The case states an assessed recommendation without supplying a general prescribing rule.

2. Why did Jules expect an antibiotic?

Answer and explanation

B. A previous illness improved after antibiotics

Jules connects improvement in an earlier illness with having taken antibiotics.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A named diagnosis or medicine instruction

The dialogue must not invent diagnostic findings or medicine instructions absent from the case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Improvement after a medicine does not, by itself, prove improvement because of the medicine. The current recommendation belongs to this assessed encounter.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
antibiotic stewardship
Responsible use of antibiotics to preserve benefit and limit avoidable harm.support antibiotic stewardship
antibiotic
Medicine used against susceptible bacterial infections.discuss an antibiotic recommendation
viral illness
Illness caused by a virus.explain a viral illness
bacterial infection
Infection caused by bacteria.assess a suspected bacterial infection
self-limiting illness
Illness that can resolve without a treatment directed at its cause.explain a self-limiting illness
symptom relief
Reduction of an uncomfortable symptom.discuss symptom relief
antimicrobial resistance
Ability of microbes to withstand medicines intended to act against them.reduce antimicrobial resistance
treatment indication
Clinical reason supporting use of a particular treatment.establish a treatment indication
watchful waiting
Planned observation with defined reassessment rather than immediate intervention.explain watchful waiting
return precautions
Instructions about changes requiring further medical attention.explain return precautions
therapeutic relationship
Working relationship supporting care between clinician and patient.maintain the therapeutic relationship
treatment expectation
What a patient anticipates receiving or achieving from treatment.explore the treatment expectation
expected benefit
Improvement a treatment may produce in a relevant situation.explain the expected benefit
potential harm
Possible adverse consequence of an action or treatment.discuss potential harm
reassessment
Further evaluation after time or a change in circumstances.arrange clinical reassessment
examination finding
Observation established through a clinical examination.explain an examination finding
diagnostic threshold
Evidence level used in deciding whether a diagnosis is sufficiently supported.clarify a diagnostic threshold
contingency plan
Agreed response if the situation develops differently from expected.agree on a contingency plan
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Not prescribing an antibiotic is not the same as offering no care. Actual symptom advice, reassessment arrangements, and warning signs must come from the treating clinician, not an invented exercise protocol.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge the expectation before explaining disagreement

The patient may be asking for relief, reassurance, or a way to return to work. Address that need without promising an unnecessary treatment or making the conversation a contest.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge experience
I can see why your previous experience shaped your expectation.
State the recommendation
Based on today's assessment, I am not recommending an antibiotic.
Explain the distinction
Improving afterward does not prove the medicine caused the improvement.
Keep the need visible
Your need for relief still matters.
Invite the concern
What worries you most about this recommendation?
Avoid a contest
Let us focus on what is likely to help in this situation.
Explain ongoing care
We still need an appropriate plan for symptoms and follow-up.
Check the contingency
Let us confirm what changes mean you should seek further help.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
After versus because of
After gives sequence; because of asserts a cause. They are not interchangeable evidence claims.
Based on today's assessment
Locates the recommendation in this encounter rather than presenting an absolute rule.
Still matters
Acknowledges the patient's goal even when the requested treatment is not recommended.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence explains the recommendation respectfully?

Answer and explanation

C. I understand the expectation; today's assessment does not support an antibiotic.

Acknowledging the expectation respects experience while retaining the recommendation based on today's assessment.

2. Which inference is unjustified?

Answer and explanation

C. Improvement afterward proves that antibiotics caused it.

Timing alone cannot establish that a medicine caused an earlier recovery.

3. What does no antibiotic mean here?

Answer and explanation

C. That particular treatment is not recommended

The recommendation concerns this treatment in this assessed visit, not all care or future illness.

4. Which closing is most useful?

Answer and explanation

B. Confirm the individualized follow-up and warning-sign plan.

A clear follow-up and safety-net plan supports care without promising that circumstances cannot change.

E Conversations

A recommendation without a dismissal

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Rao: General practitioner / Jules: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • treatment expectation
  • treatment indication
  • return precautions
  • antibiotic stewardship
  • contingency plan
  • expected benefit
  • Reassessment
  • potential harm
  • Antimicrobial resistance
  • symptom relief
  1. Jules
    Last time I felt like this, antibiotics helped. I expected to leave with the same treatment today.
  2. Dr Rao
    I understand that 1. Based on today's assessment, I am not recommending an antibiotic.
  3. Jules
    Are you saying the improvement last time was not real? I remember feeling much better afterward.
  4. Dr Rao
    The improvement was real to you. But improvement afterward does not establish the medicine's 2 in this situation.
  5. Jules
    I mainly need to feel better and get back to work. I do not want to be dismissed.
  6. Dr Rao
    Your need for 3 matters. Not recommending this medicine is not the same as offering no care.
  7. Jules
    Then please explain what separates my request from a clinical reason to prescribe the medicine.
  8. Dr Rao
    A 4 depends on the current assessment and evidence, not only on an earlier prescription.
  9. Jules
    Is the concern just about avoiding unnecessary cost, or can an unnecessary medicine cause problems too?
  10. Dr Rao
    There can be 5, as well as a lack of benefit. We should discuss that honestly without overstating either.
  11. Jules
    I have heard people say their bodies become resistant. Is that the right way to describe it?
  12. Dr Rao
    6 concerns microbes that withstand medicines. It is not your body becoming resistant to treatment.
  13. Jules
    I follow that distinction. I still want to know what happens if the illness does not develop as expected.
  14. Dr Rao
    That needs a clear 7, including the appropriate route for further help and reassessment.
  15. Jules
    Please make that specific to me rather than just saying to come back if I am worried.
  16. Dr Rao
    We will discuss individualized 8 and check that the instructions and contact route are clear.
  17. Jules
    Then a later review would not mean that I had failed to follow today's recommendation?
  18. Dr Rao
    Correct. 9 responds to the clinical situation; it is not a judgment about you.
  19. Jules
    I can accept a different recommendation when I understand the reasoning and know the next step.
  20. Dr Rao
    That is the aim of 10 alongside good care: explain the decision, address your needs, and keep follow-up clear.

Answers and explanations
  1. treatment expectation Turn 2. Treatment expectation describes what Jules anticipated based on a previous experience, not an indication established today.
  2. expected benefit Turn 4. Expected benefit concerns what a treatment may actually achieve, which cannot be proved by timing alone.
  3. symptom relief Turn 6. Symptom relief means reducing discomfort and remains a care goal even when an antibiotic is not indicated.
  4. treatment indication Turn 8. A treatment indication is a clinical reason supporting a particular treatment, not simply a request or past timing.
  5. potential harm Turn 10. Potential harm means possible adverse consequences that belong in a balanced treatment explanation.
  6. Antimicrobial resistance Turn 12. Antimicrobial resistance concerns microbes withstanding medicines, not a person's body becoming resistant to care.
  7. contingency plan Turn 14. A contingency plan describes what to do if the situation changes rather than assuming the initial course will continue.
  8. return precautions Turn 16. Return precautions identify changes requiring further medical attention and must be provided for the actual clinical situation.
  9. Reassessment Turn 18. Reassessment is a further clinical evaluation when time or changed circumstances makes it appropriate.
  10. antibiotic stewardship Turn 20. Antibiotic stewardship supports appropriate use while retaining attention to benefit, harm, and the patient's care needs.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jules: Last time I felt like this, antibiotics helped. I expected to leave with the same treatment today.
  2. Dr Rao: I understand that treatment expectation. Based on today's assessment, I am not recommending an antibiotic.
  3. Jules: Are you saying the improvement last time was not real? I remember feeling much better afterward.
  4. Dr Rao: The improvement was real to you. But improvement afterward does not establish the medicine's expected benefit in this situation.
  5. Jules: I mainly need to feel better and get back to work. I do not want to be dismissed.
  6. Dr Rao: Your need for symptom relief matters. Not recommending this medicine is not the same as offering no care.
  7. Jules: Then please explain what separates my request from a clinical reason to prescribe the medicine.
  8. Dr Rao: A treatment indication depends on the current assessment and evidence, not only on an earlier prescription.
  9. Jules: Is the concern just about avoiding unnecessary cost, or can an unnecessary medicine cause problems too?
  10. Dr Rao: There can be potential harm, as well as a lack of benefit. We should discuss that honestly without overstating either.
  11. Jules: I have heard people say their bodies become resistant. Is that the right way to describe it?
  12. Dr Rao: Antimicrobial resistance concerns microbes that withstand medicines. It is not your body becoming resistant to treatment.
  13. Jules: I follow that distinction. I still want to know what happens if the illness does not develop as expected.
  14. Dr Rao: That needs a clear contingency plan, including the appropriate route for further help and reassessment.
  15. Jules: Please make that specific to me rather than just saying to come back if I am worried.
  16. Dr Rao: We will discuss individualized return precautions and check that the instructions and contact route are clear.
  17. Jules: Then a later review would not mean that I had failed to follow today's recommendation?
  18. Dr Rao: Correct. Reassessment responds to the clinical situation; it is not a judgment about you.
  19. Jules: I can accept a different recommendation when I understand the reasoning and know the next step.
  20. Dr Rao: That is the aim of antibiotic stewardship alongside good care: explain the decision, address your needs, and keep follow-up clear.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A previous improvement is not proof of cause

A patient links an antibiotic to recovery in an earlier illness.

  1. Patient: I improved after antibiotics last time, so I assumed they caused the recovery.
  2. GP: Improvement afterward describes the sequence, but it does not by itself prove the cause.
  3. Patient: Are you saying that my memory of getting better is wrong?
  4. GP: No. The improvement can be real while its explanation still needs careful interpretation.
  5. Patient: I want to understand today's recommendation rather than repeat last year's decision automatically.
  6. GP: That is the right question: today's assessment and indication determine the recommendation.
  7. Patient: Could you explain the reasoning without making me feel foolish for asking?
  8. GP: Certainly. We should discuss the actual findings, expected benefit, and possible harms.
  9. Patient: I also need the follow-up advice that belongs to today's clinical plan.
  10. GP: We will explain that plan and its contact route without assuming every similar symptom has the same cause.
A prescription request from a colleague

Two clinicians clarify the basis of an antibiotic request.

  1. Clinician: The patient asked whether we could repeat the antibiotic from a previous encounter.
  2. GP: What is the current assessment and indication, rather than the earlier prescription alone?
  3. Clinician: The request is documented, but the clinical decision has not yet been made.
  4. GP: Then please keep patient request separate from clinician recommendation in the record.
  5. Clinician: I should not write antibiotic required simply because the patient asked for one.
  6. GP: Correct. That wording would convert a request into a conclusion unsupported by the assessment.
  7. Clinician: Would you like the previous reaction history included in the review as well?
  8. GP: Yes, with its source and certainty clear, alongside the actual current findings.
  9. Clinician: I will send the verified information and identify what is still unknown.
  10. GP: Then the responsible clinician can explain the decision using evidence, not a copied instruction.
Understanding a no-antibiotic recommendation

A patient checks the explanation of an assessed recommendation.

  1. GP: After the actual assessment, my recommendation is not to use an antibiotic for this episode.
  2. Patient: I would like to understand that recommendation, not just hear that the answer is no.
  3. GP: Let us discuss the assessed reason and what benefit an antibiotic would or would not offer here.
  4. Patient: Does the recommendation mean that my symptoms are imaginary or unimportant?
  5. GP: No. Taking your symptoms seriously and recommending an antibiotic are not the same thing.
  6. Patient: I also want to know what the actual care plan says if the situation changes.
  7. GP: We will review the relevant follow-up instructions and the appropriate contact route.
  8. Patient: Please check that I have understood the plan rather than assuming my nod means agreement.
  9. GP: Tell me how you understand the recommendation, and I will clarify anything I explained poorly.
  10. Patient: Then I can distinguish the treatment decision from whether my concern has been heard.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A recommendation without a dismissal
  1. Read the corrected recommendation. Stress after and because of to distinguish sequence from cause.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the acknowledgment, recommendation, and follow-up lines without turning them into a prescribing rule.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain a different treatment expectation

Noor requests a scan because a relative received one. Dr Hale has assessed Noor and does not recommend that scan at this visit. The relative's case is not available. Follow-up remains part of care.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Noor: "My ___ received a scan."

Answer and explanation

A. relative

The request is influenced by a relative's experience, not a supplied scan indication.

2. Dr Hale: "Your assessment does not support that scan ___."

Answer and explanation

C. today

The recommendation belongs to today's assessed encounter, not every future situation.

3. Noor: "The other person's clinical details are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unavailable

The scenario does not supply the relative's history or reasons for imaging.

4. Dr Hale: "We still need clear ___."

Answer and explanation

C. follow-up

Not recommending the requested scan does not remove the need for appropriate follow-up.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Original fictional case: After an assessment, Dr Rao has not identified an indication for an antibiotic in this fictional visit. Language guidance: Improvement after a medicine does not, by itself, prove improvement because of the medicine. The current recommendation belongs to this assessed encounter.

Illness caused by a virus. / bacterial infection: Infection caused by bacteria. / self-limiting illness: Illness that can resolve without a treatment directed at its cause. / symptom relief: Reduction of an uncomfortable symptom. / antimicrobial resistance: Ability of microbes to withstand medicines intended to act against them. / treatment indication: Clinical reason supporting use of a particular treatment. / watchful waiting: Planned observation with defined reassessment rather than immediate intervention. / return precautions: Instructions about changes requiring further medical attention. / therapeutic relationship: Working relationship supporting care between clinician and patient. / treatment expectation: What a patient anticipates receiving or achieving from treatment. / expected benefit: Improvement a treatment may produce in a relevant situation. / potential harm: Possible adverse consequence of an action or treatment. / reassessment: Further evaluation after time or a change in circumstances. / examination finding: Observation established through a clinical examination. / diagnostic threshold: Evidence level used in deciding whether a diagnosis is sufficiently supported. / contingency plan: Agreed response if the situation develops differently from expected. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

treatment. / watchful waiting: Planned observation with defined reassessment rather than immediate intervention. / return precautions: Instructions about changes requiring further medical attention. / therapeutic relationship: Working relationship supporting care between clinician and patient. / treatment expectation: What a patient anticipates receiving or achieving from treatment. / expected benefit: Improvement a treatment may produce in a relevant situation. / potential harm: Possible adverse consequence of an action or treatment. / reassessment: Further evaluation after time or a change in circumstances. / examination finding: Observation established through a clinical examination. / diagnostic threshold: Evidence level used in deciding whether a diagnosis is sufficiently supported. / contingency plan: Agreed response if the situation develops differently from expected. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected recommendation. Stress after and because of to distinguish sequence from cause. Swap roles. Repeat the acknowledgment, recommendation, and follow-up lines without turning them into a prescribing rule. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Rao: General practitioner / Jules: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Noor requests a scan because a relative received one. Dr Hale has assessed Noor and does not recommend that scan at this visit. The relative's case is not available. Follow-up remains part of care.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jules: Then a later review would not mean that I had failed to follow today's recommendation?
Dr Rao: Correct. Reassessment responds to the clinical situation; it is not a judgment about you.
Jules: I can accept a different recommendation when I understand the reasoning and know the next step.
Dr Rao: That is the aim of antibiotic stewardship alongside good care: explain the decision, address your needs, and keep follow-up clear.
END REFERENCE
06Discussing preventive screening choicesAn invitation is not a diagnosis+

Your goal

Compare screening benefits and limitations while preserving a voluntary, informed choice.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

An invitation is not a diagnosis

Dana receives a screening invitation and asks Dr Ellis whether it means a disease is suspected. The invitation is not a diagnostic result. Eligibility and the appropriate options need an individual review; no screening schedule or personal risk calculation is supplied. Dana wants to understand possible benefit, false alarms, and subsequent testing before choosing.

Dr Ellis: General practitioner / Dana: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What prompted the discussion?

Answer and explanation

C. A screening invitation

The supplied document is an invitation rather than a result or diagnosis.

2. What does Dana want explained?

Answer and explanation

A. Benefits, false alarms, and subsequent testing

Dana asks about the tradeoffs and next steps before deciding.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. An individual screening schedule

The exercise does not establish eligibility, an interval, or an individual clinical recommendation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Screening and diagnostic testing serve different purposes. A screening invitation is not evidence that disease has been detected.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
screening
Testing intended to identify possible disease or risk before relevant symptoms prompt diagnosis.discuss screening options
diagnostic testing
Investigation intended to establish or explain a suspected clinical condition.distinguish diagnostic testing
average risk
Risk category without specified factors that would place someone in a higher category.assess average-risk eligibility
shared decision-making
Combining evidence and patient priorities in a care decision.support shared decision-making
absolute risk
Chance of an outcome over a stated period in a defined group.explain absolute risk
relative risk
Risk in one group compared with risk in another group.distinguish relative risk
false alarm
Result suggesting a problem that further investigation does not confirm.explain a possible false alarm
overdiagnosis
Detection of a condition that would not have caused harm during a person's lifetime.discuss overdiagnosis
eligibility
Whether a person meets the criteria for an option or service.review screening eligibility
prevention
Action aimed at reducing disease occurrence or its consequences.discuss preventive care
asymptomatic
Without the relevant symptoms being considered.describe an asymptomatic population
positive screen
Screening result indicating that further evaluation may be needed.explain a positive screen
screening interval
Time between planned screening episodes.review the screening interval
risk factor
Characteristic associated with a change in likelihood of an outcome.identify a relevant risk factor
family history
Health information about a person's biological relatives.update the family history
informed choice
Decision made with adequate understanding of relevant options and consequences.support an informed choice
decision aid
Resource helping a person compare care options and their tradeoffs.use a decision aid
follow-up test
Further investigation after an earlier test or finding.explain a follow-up test
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A risk percentage needs an outcome, time period, and comparison group. Do not invent those details or use a relative change as though it were an absolute chance.

C Notice the language

Discuss the tradeoff without steering through fear

Explain what a screening test can and cannot establish. A useful discussion includes the person's priorities and practical implications, not just a slogan that testing is always better.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Explain the invitation
This invites a discussion; it does not announce a diagnosis.
Separate purposes
Screening and testing a symptom are different situations.
Explain an uncertain result
A positive screen may lead to further evaluation.
Name the tradeoff
We should discuss possible benefit as well as false alarms.
Ask about priorities
Which parts of the decision matter most to you?
Clarify a number
Over what time period does that risk apply?
Protect choice
You can ask questions before deciding.
Review eligibility
We need to check which options fit your circumstances.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
May lead to
Describes a possible consequence rather than an inevitable result.
Rather than
Makes the contrast between screening and diagnosis explicit.
Over what period
Requests a missing time horizon needed to interpret a risk statement.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What does the invitation establish?

Answer and explanation

A. An opportunity to discuss screening

The invitation itself does not establish disease, results, or universal eligibility.

2. Which explanation of a positive screen is safest?

Answer and explanation

D. It may require further evaluation; it is not automatically a diagnosis.

Screening results require appropriate interpretation and may lead to diagnostic evaluation.

3. Which risk statement is incomplete?

Answer and explanation

A. The risk is lower, without an outcome or time period.

A meaningful risk comparison requires the event, population, and time period being compared.

4. What supports informed choice?

Answer and explanation

A. Explain benefits, limitations, and next steps, then elicit priorities.

An informed choice requires relevant information and attention to the person's priorities.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

An invitation is not a diagnosis

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Ellis: General practitioner / Dana: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Absolute risk
  • Diagnostic testing
  • false alarm
  • shared decision-making
  • eligibility
  • informed choice
  • positive screen
  • overdiagnosis
  • asymptomatic
  • screening
  1. Dana
    This letter invited me for screening. Does that mean someone thinks I already have the disease?
  2. Dr Ellis
    An invitation to 1 does not announce a diagnosis. Let us review what the invitation offers.
  3. Dana
    I do not have the symptom mentioned in an article I read. Is that a contradiction?
  4. Dr Ellis
    Not necessarily. Screening often concerns an 2 population; investigating a symptom is a different clinical situation.
  5. Dana
    Then the test in this letter is not the same as testing to explain a current complaint?
  6. Dr Ellis
    Correct. 3 and screening have different purposes, although one can lead to the other.
  7. Dana
    Before deciding, I want to know what a worrying result would mean and what would happen afterward.
  8. Dr Ellis
    A 4 may lead to further evaluation. It does not automatically confirm the condition.
  9. Dana
    Could I go through more tests and then discover that the original concern was not confirmed?
  10. Dr Ellis
    Yes, a 5 is one limitation to explain alongside the possible benefits and other consequences.
  11. Dana
    People also talk about finding conditions that would never have affected them. Is that the same issue?
  12. Dr Ellis
    That is 6, which differs from a false alarm. We should explain the distinction without assuming either will happen to you.
  13. Dana
    I would like the numbers explained carefully. A headline saying lower risk does not tell me much.
  14. Dr Ellis
    We need the event and time period. 7 is not the same thing as a relative comparison.
  15. Dana
    And you still need to check whether this particular screening option applies to my circumstances?
  16. Dr Ellis
    Yes. We must review 8 and your history rather than treating a general invitation as the entire assessment.
  17. Dana
    I want to compare the options without feeling that asking about limitations means I am refusing care.
  18. Dr Ellis
    Questions support 9. Your priorities and the evidence both belong in the discussion.
  19. Dana
    That gives me a clearer starting point. I understand this is an invitation, not a diagnosis or a guarantee.
  20. Dr Ellis
    Exactly. We can use the relevant information to support an 10, then agree on any next step.

Answers and explanations
  1. screening Turn 2. Screening seeks possible disease or risk and an invitation does not itself establish a diagnosis.
  2. asymptomatic Turn 4. Asymptomatic means without the relevant symptoms, which is different from proving that no condition is present.
  3. Diagnostic testing Turn 6. Diagnostic testing seeks to establish or explain a suspected clinical condition rather than merely inviting a screening discussion.
  4. positive screen Turn 8. A positive screen indicates a possible concern requiring evaluation and is not automatically a confirmed diagnosis.
  5. false alarm Turn 10. A false alarm suggests a problem that subsequent investigation does not confirm, one possible limitation to discuss.
  6. overdiagnosis Turn 12. Overdiagnosis is detecting a condition that would not have caused harm during the person's lifetime, distinct from a false alarm.
  7. Absolute risk Turn 14. Absolute risk describes the chance of an outcome over a defined period, rather than only a ratio between groups.
  8. eligibility Turn 16. Eligibility means meeting the criteria for the relevant option and is not established solely by reading a general invitation.
  9. shared decision-making Turn 18. Shared decision-making combines relevant evidence with the person's priorities rather than substituting pressure for discussion.
  10. informed choice Turn 20. An informed choice follows an adequate explanation of options and consequences and respects the person's decision.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dana: This letter invited me for screening. Does that mean someone thinks I already have the disease?
  2. Dr Ellis: An invitation to screening does not announce a diagnosis. Let us review what the invitation offers.
  3. Dana: I do not have the symptom mentioned in an article I read. Is that a contradiction?
  4. Dr Ellis: Not necessarily. Screening often concerns an asymptomatic population; investigating a symptom is a different clinical situation.
  5. Dana: Then the test in this letter is not the same as testing to explain a current complaint?
  6. Dr Ellis: Correct. Diagnostic testing and screening have different purposes, although one can lead to the other.
  7. Dana: Before deciding, I want to know what a worrying result would mean and what would happen afterward.
  8. Dr Ellis: A positive screen may lead to further evaluation. It does not automatically confirm the condition.
  9. Dana: Could I go through more tests and then discover that the original concern was not confirmed?
  10. Dr Ellis: Yes, a false alarm is one limitation to explain alongside the possible benefits and other consequences.
  11. Dana: People also talk about finding conditions that would never have affected them. Is that the same issue?
  12. Dr Ellis: That is overdiagnosis, which differs from a false alarm. We should explain the distinction without assuming either will happen to you.
  13. Dana: I would like the numbers explained carefully. A headline saying lower risk does not tell me much.
  14. Dr Ellis: We need the event and time period. Absolute risk is not the same thing as a relative comparison.
  15. Dana: And you still need to check whether this particular screening option applies to my circumstances?
  16. Dr Ellis: Yes. We must review eligibility and your history rather than treating a general invitation as the entire assessment.
  17. Dana: I want to compare the options without feeling that asking about limitations means I am refusing care.
  18. Dr Ellis: Questions support shared decision-making. Your priorities and the evidence both belong in the discussion.
  19. Dana: That gives me a clearer starting point. I understand this is an invitation, not a diagnosis or a guarantee.
  20. Dr Ellis: Exactly. We can use the relevant information to support an informed choice, then agree on any next step.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An invitation is not a diagnosis

A patient receives a screening invitation.

  1. Patient: The screening invitation worried me because I thought the service had already found something wrong.
  2. GP: An invitation offers a screening discussion; it is not a diagnosis or an abnormal result.
  3. Patient: Then the first question is what this particular screening test is intended to detect?
  4. GP: Yes, along with its possible benefits, limitations, and the choices available to you.
  5. Patient: I would like to know what could happen after either a positive or negative result.
  6. GP: Those pathways belong in the explanation, using the actual screening program.
  7. Patient: Does accepting the invitation mean I have agreed to every possible later procedure?
  8. GP: No. Later decisions may require their own information and appropriate consent discussion.
  9. Patient: I appreciate separating the invitation, the test, and any later diagnostic assessment.
  10. GP: That sequence helps you make an informed choice without assuming the result in advance.
A false alarm and overdiagnosis differ

A clinician explains two different screening limitations.

  1. GP: You asked whether a false-positive result and overdiagnosis mean the same thing.
  2. Patient: I assumed both meant that the test found something that was not really there.
  3. GP: A false positive is not confirmed as the condition being assessed.
  4. Patient: So overdiagnosis must refer to a different problem than an unconfirmed alarm?
  5. GP: Yes. It concerns detecting a real condition that would not have caused relevant harm.
  6. Patient: That sounds different because the finding may be real even when detection does not help.
  7. GP: Exactly. The terms describe different limitations and should not be used interchangeably.
  8. Patient: Can we discuss their relevance to the actual screening option rather than every test together?
  9. GP: We should use the particular program's evidence and explain the uncertainty honestly.
  10. Patient: Then I can compare the possible benefits and harms using the correct meanings.
Declining today is not refusing all future care

A patient postpones a screening choice.

  1. Patient: I do not want to decide on screening today, but I am not refusing every future discussion.
  2. GP: I will record that accurately rather than describe your choice as a blanket refusal.
  3. Patient: I need the information in a form I can understand before making a decision.
  4. GP: We can review the actual option, its limitations, and any relevant timing considerations.
  5. Patient: Could you also explain whether the invitation is screening or follow-up for an existing concern?
  6. GP: That distinction matters, and we should verify which process this invitation belongs to.
  7. Patient: I do not want a postponement recorded as though the test was already completed.
  8. GP: It should not be. Offered, accepted, declined, and completed are different statuses.
  9. Patient: Once the actual information is clear, we can discuss the next step appropriately.
  10. GP: Yes. The record should reflect the decision actually made, not one inferred from silence.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An invitation is not a diagnosis
  1. Read the corrected screening exchange. Contrast screening, positive screen, and diagnosis.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the distinction between false alarm and overdiagnosis, preserving the uncertainty.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify a population statistic

A leaflet gives a risk comparison for a defined study population over five years. Kim asks whether it is a personal prediction. Dr Reed explains that individual interpretation requires clinical context.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Kim: "The leaflet describes a study ___."

Answer and explanation

C. population

The figure concerns a defined study group, not a guaranteed individual outcome.

2. Dr Reed: "Its time horizon is five ___."

Answer and explanation

D. years

Five years is the explicit period attached to the comparison.

3. Kim: "It is not a guaranteed personal ___."

Answer and explanation

C. prediction

A population statistic does not determine exactly what will happen to Kim.

4. Dr Reed: "Individual interpretation needs clinical ___."

Answer and explanation

D. context

The scenario states that personal interpretation requires the relevant clinical circumstances.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Professional audience: general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A leaflet gives a risk comparison for a defined study population over five years. Kim asks whether it is a personal prediction. Dr Reed explains that individual interpretation requires clinical context.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: I want to compare the options without feeling that asking about limitations means I am refusing care.
Dr Ellis: Questions support shared decision-making. Your priorities and the evidence both belong in the discussion.
Dana: That gives me a clearer starting point. I understand this is an invitation, not a diagnosis or a guarantee.
Dr Ellis: Exactly. We can use the relevant information to support an informed choice, then agree on any next step.
