Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: General Practice English
Professional audience: general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Lesson 06: Discussing preventive screening choices
Original fictional case: Dana receives a screening invitation and asks Dr Ellis whether it means a disease is suspected. The invitation is not a diagnostic result. Eligibility and the appropriate options need an individual review; no screening schedule or personal risk calculation is supplied. Dana wants to understand possible benefit, false alarms, and subsequent testing before choosing.
Language workshop: An invitation is not a diagnosis
Communication goal: Compare screening benefits and limitations while preserving a voluntary, informed choice.
Language guidance: Screening and diagnostic testing serve different purposes. A screening invitation is not evidence that disease has been detected.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This invites a discussion; it does not announce a diagnosis. / Screening and testing a symptom are different situations. / A positive screen may lead to further evaluation. / We should discuss possible benefit as well as false alarms. / Which parts of the decision matter most to you? / Over what time period does that risk apply? / You can ask questions before deciding. / We need to check which options fit your circumstances. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: screening: Testing intended to identify possible disease or risk before relevant symptoms prompt diagnosis. / diagnostic testing: Investigation intended to establish or explain a suspected clinical condition. / average risk: Risk category without specified factors that would place someone in a higher category. / shared decision-making: Combining evidence and patient priorities in a care decision. / absolute risk: Chance of an outcome over a stated period in a defined group. / relative risk: Risk in one group compared with risk in another group. / false alarm: Result suggesting a problem that further investigation does not confirm. / overdiagnosis: Detection of a condition that would not have caused harm during a person's lifetime. / eligibility: Whether a person meets the criteria for an option or service. / prevention: Action aimed at reducing disease occurrence or its consequences. / asymptomatic: Without the relevant symptoms being considered. / positive screen: Screening result indicating that further evaluation may be needed. / screening interval: Time between planned screening episodes. / risk factor: Characteristic associated with a change in likelihood of an outcome. / family history: Health information about a person's biological relatives. / informed choice: Decision made with adequate understanding of relevant options and consequences. / decision aid: Resource helping a person compare care options and their tradeoffs. / follow-up test: Further investigation after an earlier test or finding. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected screening exchange. Contrast screening, positive screen, and diagnosis. Swap roles. Repeat the distinction between false alarm and overdiagnosis, preserving the uncertainty. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Ellis: General practitioner / Dana: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A leaflet gives a risk comparison for a defined study population over five years. Kim asks whether it is a personal prediction. Dr Reed explains that individual interpretation requires clinical context.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: I want to compare the options without feeling that asking about limitations means I am refusing care.
Dr Ellis: Questions support shared decision-making. Your priorities and the evidence both belong in the discussion.
Dana: That gives me a clearer starting point. I understand this is an invitation, not a diagnosis or a guarantee.
Dr Ellis: Exactly. We can use the relevant information to support an informed choice, then agree on any next step.

07Opening a mental-health conversationMake a direct question feel safe+

Your goal

Ask sensitively about mood and safety while explaining confidentiality limits and a warm handoff.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Make a direct question feel safe

Chris reports low mood and poor sleep affecting work. Dr Wells will ask about safety directly rather than infer it from appearance. No risk assessment has been completed and no risk level is supplied. A behavioral-health colleague is available for an introduction if appropriate and agreed. Dr Wells must explain relevant confidentiality limits accurately rather than promise absolute secrecy.

Dr Wells: General practitioner / Chris: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What impact does Chris report?

Answer and explanation

D. Mood and sleep difficulties affecting work

The case describes reported difficulties and their effect, not a completed diagnosis.

2. What remains incomplete?

Answer and explanation

A. The risk assessment

No risk assessment or risk level has been established in the brief.

3. What must not be promised?

Answer and explanation

A. Absolute secrecy

Confidentiality has limits that depend on the actual situation and applicable requirements.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A calm appearance does not establish low risk. Ask and assess directly; do not let an English exercise stand in for an actual safety evaluation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
low mood
Persistent or recurrent feelings of sadness or reduced emotional well-being.explore low mood
anhedonia
Reduced ability to experience interest or pleasure.ask about anhedonia
sleep disturbance
Disruption of the usual pattern or quality of sleep.describe sleep disturbance
stressor
Circumstance placing psychological or practical demands on a person.identify a current stressor
protective factor
Circumstance or resource that may reduce vulnerability to harm.explore protective factors
risk assessment
Clinical evaluation of relevant risks and protective circumstances.complete a risk assessment
suicidal thoughts
Thoughts about ending one's own life.ask directly about suicidal thoughts
psychosocial history
Account of psychological and social circumstances relevant to care.take a psychosocial history
nonjudgmental language
Wording that explores experience without assigning blame or moral fault.use nonjudgmental language
behavioral health
Care addressing mental health, behavior, and related well-being.coordinate behavioral-health care
warm handoff
Supported introduction to another care professional with relevant context.arrange a warm handoff
confidentiality limit
Circumstance in which privacy cannot be guaranteed under applicable duties.explain confidentiality limits
crisis response
Immediate professional response to an urgent safety concern.activate the appropriate crisis response
distress
Significant emotional suffering or discomfort.acknowledge distress
daily functioning
Ability to manage ordinary work, self-care, and other activities.assess daily functioning
support network
People or services available to provide help.identify a support network
consent to share
Agreement to disclose specified information in an appropriate context.clarify consent to share
therapeutic alliance
Collaborative relationship supporting care and agreed goals.strengthen the therapeutic alliance
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A warm handoff is more than passing along a telephone number. Explain the introduction and information sharing, following actual consent and safety requirements.

C Notice the language

Direct can also be compassionate

A clear safety question need not be abrupt. Explain why you are asking, listen without judgment, and avoid making disclosure sound like a test the patient can fail.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open sensitively
How have things been emotionally as well as physically?
Ask directly
Have you had thoughts about ending your life?
Explain the question
I ask directly because your safety matters.
Avoid an assumption
I cannot judge that from how you look today.
Explain privacy limits
I will explain when I may need to involve others for safety.
Offer an introduction
We can discuss an introduction to our behavioral-health colleague.
Clarify information sharing
Let us agree what information is shared, within the applicable duties.
Keep the person involved
I will explain the next steps as clearly as I can.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Have you had
The present perfect invites experience over a relevant period rather than assuming a current answer.
May need to
Explains a possible duty without promising secrecy or announcing a decision already made.
From how you look
Identifies an inadequate basis for a risk conclusion.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which safety question is direct and neutral?

Answer and explanation

A. Have you had thoughts about ending your life?

The direct question invites an honest answer without shame or an assumed conclusion.

2. Which privacy statement is inappropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. I promise absolute secrecy in every circumstance.

Absolute secrecy may conflict with applicable safety and legal duties.

3. What does a warm handoff add?

Answer and explanation

D. A supported introduction with relevant context

A warm handoff connects care while preserving appropriate consent and information boundaries.

4. Which inference must be avoided?

Answer and explanation

B. A calm appearance proves low risk.

Appearance alone cannot establish a person's safety or replace a risk assessment.

E Conversations

Make a direct question feel safe

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Wells: General practitioner / Chris: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • support network
  • nonjudgmental language
  • consent to share
  • low mood
  • warm handoff
  • confidentiality limits
  • anhedonia
  • suicidal thoughts
  • daily functioning
  • risk assessment
  1. Dr Wells
    You mentioned poor sleep and difficulty at work. How have things been emotionally as well as physically?
  2. Chris
    My 1 has been harder to manage than I expected, but I struggle to explain it.
  3. Dr Wells
    We can take it one part at a time. What has changed in your usual activities or interests?
  4. Chris
    I stopped enjoying things I normally like. I saw the word 2, but I was unsure how to say it.
  5. Dr Wells
    Your own description is enough. I also need to ask directly about your safety rather than make assumptions.
  6. Chris
    I was worried that my 3 would look normal because I managed to come to this appointment.
  7. Dr Wells
    Coming here does not tell me everything. Have you had thoughts about ending your life?
  8. Chris
    That is difficult to discuss. Will talking about 4 mean that everything I say is automatically shared with everyone?
  9. Dr Wells
    No. I will explain privacy and its limits, including when safety duties may require involving other people.
  10. Chris
    Please explain those 5 before assuming I understand them. I want to answer honestly.
  11. Dr Wells
    Of course. We will discuss the relevant rules and your situation, and I will explain the next steps.
  12. Chris
    I appreciate the 6. I was afraid this would become a lecture about coping better.
  13. Dr Wells
    My aim is to understand what is happening and assess safety, including difficulties and sources of support.
  14. Chris
    My sister has been helpful. I would like her considered as part of my 7.
  15. Dr Wells
    We can discuss that. Our behavioral-health colleague is also available, depending on the assessed needs and agreed plan.
  16. Chris
    Could you introduce us? A 8 would be easier than repeating everything to a stranger without preparation.
  17. Dr Wells
    We can discuss the introduction and what information is appropriate to share, subject to the relevant duties.
  18. Chris
    Then my 9 is something we clarify rather than assume from my attendance here.
  19. Dr Wells
    Yes. We must also complete the safety assessment and respond appropriately to anything urgent that it reveals.
  20. Chris
    I understand that the 10 has not already been completed just because I have started talking.

Answers and explanations
  1. low mood Turn 2. Low mood describes Chris's reported emotional difficulty without itself establishing a specific diagnosis.
  2. anhedonia Turn 4. Anhedonia refers to reduced interest or pleasure, which can be explored without diagnosing from the word alone.
  3. daily functioning Turn 6. Daily functioning concerns ordinary activities such as work and self-care, not just how someone appears in a consultation.
  4. suicidal thoughts Turn 8. Suicidal thoughts concern ending one's life and require direct, sensitive clinical assessment rather than assumptions.
  5. confidentiality limits Turn 10. Confidentiality limits are circumstances where applicable duties prevent a promise of absolute secrecy.
  6. nonjudgmental language Turn 12. Nonjudgmental language explores the person's experience without assigning blame or shaming a disclosure.
  7. support network Turn 14. A support network consists of people or services that can help, while their involvement still needs appropriate discussion.
  8. warm handoff Turn 16. A warm handoff provides a supported introduction with context rather than leaving the patient with only a contact number.
  9. consent to share Turn 18. Consent to share concerns permission for specified information, within the applicable safety and legal requirements.
  10. risk assessment Turn 20. The risk assessment is still required and cannot be replaced by a calm appearance or a promised referral.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Wells: You mentioned poor sleep and difficulty at work. How have things been emotionally as well as physically?
  2. Chris: My low mood has been harder to manage than I expected, but I struggle to explain it.
  3. Dr Wells: We can take it one part at a time. What has changed in your usual activities or interests?
  4. Chris: I stopped enjoying things I normally like. I saw the word anhedonia, but I was unsure how to say it.
  5. Dr Wells: Your own description is enough. I also need to ask directly about your safety rather than make assumptions.
  6. Chris: I was worried that my daily functioning would look normal because I managed to come to this appointment.
  7. Dr Wells: Coming here does not tell me everything. Have you had thoughts about ending your life?
  8. Chris: That is difficult to discuss. Will talking about suicidal thoughts mean that everything I say is automatically shared with everyone?
  9. Dr Wells: No. I will explain privacy and its limits, including when safety duties may require involving other people.
  10. Chris: Please explain those confidentiality limits before assuming I understand them. I want to answer honestly.
  11. Dr Wells: Of course. We will discuss the relevant rules and your situation, and I will explain the next steps.
  12. Chris: I appreciate the nonjudgmental language. I was afraid this would become a lecture about coping better.
  13. Dr Wells: My aim is to understand what is happening and assess safety, including difficulties and sources of support.
  14. Chris: My sister has been helpful. I would like her considered as part of my support network.
  15. Dr Wells: We can discuss that. Our behavioral-health colleague is also available, depending on the assessed needs and agreed plan.
  16. Chris: Could you introduce us? A warm handoff would be easier than repeating everything to a stranger without preparation.
  17. Dr Wells: We can discuss the introduction and what information is appropriate to share, subject to the relevant duties.
  18. Chris: Then my consent to share is something we clarify rather than assume from my attendance here.
  19. Dr Wells: Yes. We must also complete the safety assessment and respond appropriately to anything urgent that it reveals.
  20. Chris: I understand that the risk assessment has not already been completed just because I have started talking.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A direct safety question

A clinician explains why a sensitive question is being asked.

  1. GP: I would like to ask directly whether you have had thoughts of ending your life.
  2. Patient: I appreciate the clear wording, although it is difficult for me to answer.
  3. GP: Take the space you need within the actual assessment. The question is not a judgment.
  4. Patient: Will an honest answer automatically be reduced to a label without hearing the context?
  5. GP: Your account needs proper assessment, including relevant details and the actual safety response.
  6. Patient: I also want to understand the limits of privacy before sharing more.
  7. GP: We should explain the applicable confidentiality and safety duties rather than promise absolute secrecy.
  8. Patient: I do not want to be reassured or classified from one sentence alone.
  9. GP: That would not be sufficient. The clinical assessment and appropriate support remain essential.
  10. Patient: Clear questions and an explanation of the process make it easier to describe what is happening.
Low mood and a diagnosis are different

A patient asks how symptom language relates to assessment.

  1. Patient: I have described low mood, but I do not know whether that establishes a diagnosis.
  2. GP: A symptom report contributes to assessment; it does not replace the full clinical evaluation.
  3. Patient: I was worried that using the wrong word would make you misunderstand the seriousness.
  4. GP: Please describe your experience, its course, and its effect rather than choosing a label for me.
  5. Patient: Some days are different from others, so a single word feels incomplete.
  6. GP: That variation belongs in the account, with uncertainty preserved where you are unsure.
  7. Patient: Could we distinguish what I experience from what someone else has noticed?
  8. GP: Yes. Both can be useful, but each source should remain identifiable.
  9. Patient: I would also like to know the actual next step after this discussion.
  10. GP: We will explain the appropriate assessment and support plan without diagnosing from vocabulary alone.
A warm handoff needs acknowledgment

Two clinicians coordinate the next support contact.

  1. GP: The patient has agreed to this referral, and I am confirming the receiving contact.
  2. Receiving clinician: I can acknowledge the referral information, but we should clarify the actual next appointment arrangement.
  3. GP: The patient should not assume that sending the request means a visit is already booked.
  4. Receiving clinician: Agreed. Receipt, acceptance, and appointment confirmation need to remain separate.
  5. GP: I will include the relevant concerns and actual safety arrangements without unnecessary personal detail.
  6. Receiving clinician: Please preserve what is reported, what was assessed, and what remains unresolved.
  7. GP: Who will explain the next step to the patient and check any access difficulty?
  8. Receiving clinician: Let us assign that responsibility explicitly rather than leave it between the two services.
  9. GP: I will document the accepted handoff and the outstanding scheduling information.
  10. Receiving clinician: That closes the communication loop without pretending the whole care pathway is already complete.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Make a direct question feel safe
  1. Read the corrected safety conversation calmly, with a pause before and after the direct question.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the privacy explanation without adding an absolute promise or an invented risk level.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: General Practice English
Professional audience: general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 07: Opening a mental-health conversation
Original fictional case: Chris reports low mood and poor sleep affecting work. Dr Wells will ask about safety directly rather than infer it from appearance. No risk assessment has been completed and no risk level is supplied. A behavioral-health colleague is available for an introduction if appropriate and agreed. Dr Wells must explain relevant confidentiality limits accurately rather than promise absolute secrecy.
Language workshop: Make a direct question feel safe
Communication goal: Ask sensitively about mood and safety while explaining confidentiality limits and a warm handoff.
Language guidance: A calm appearance does not establish low risk. Ask and assess directly; do not let an English exercise stand in for an actual safety evaluation.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): How have things been emotionally as well as physically? / Have you had thoughts about ending your life? / I ask directly because your safety matters. / I cannot judge that from how you look today. / I will explain when I may need to involve others for safety. / We can discuss an introduction to our behavioral-health colleague. / Let us agree what information is shared, within the applicable duties. / I will explain the next steps as clearly as I can. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: low mood: Persistent or recurrent feelings of sadness or reduced emotional well-being. / anhedonia: Reduced ability to experience interest or pleasure. / sleep disturbance: Disruption of the usual pattern or quality of sleep. / stressor: Circumstance placing psychological or practical demands on a person. / protective factor: Circumstance or resource that may reduce vulnerability to harm. / risk assessment: Clinical evaluation of relevant risks and protective circumstances. / suicidal thoughts: Thoughts about ending one's own life. / psychosocial history: Account of psychological and social circumstances relevant to care. / nonjudgmental language: Wording that explores experience without assigning blame or moral fault. / behavioral health: Care addressing mental health, behavior, and related well-being. / warm handoff: Supported introduction to another care professional with relevant context. / confidentiality limit: Circumstance in which privacy cannot be guaranteed under applicable duties. / crisis response: Immediate professional response to an urgent safety concern. / distress: Significant emotional suffering or discomfort. / daily functioning: Ability to manage ordinary work, self-care, and other activities. / support network: People or services available to provide help. / consent to share: Agreement to disclose specified information in an appropriate context. / therapeutic alliance: Collaborative relationship supporting care and agreed goals. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected safety conversation calmly, with a pause before and after the direct question. Swap roles. Repeat the privacy explanation without adding an absolute promise or an invented risk level. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Wells: General practitioner / Chris: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Rae agrees to an introduction to counselor Jo. The relevant information to share will be clarified. Dr Moss has not completed the safety assessment; no risk level is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Wells: We can discuss the introduction and what information is appropriate to share, subject to the relevant duties.
Chris: Then my consent to share is something we clarify rather than assume from my attendance here.
Dr Wells: Yes. We must also complete the safety assessment and respond appropriately to anything urgent that it reveals.
Chris: I understand that the risk assessment has not already been completed just because I have started talking.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Closing the referral loopSent does not mean seen+

Your goal

Give a referral update that identifies the open question, owner, and next contact.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Sent does not mean seen

Dr Byrne referred Fin to a specialist for an unresolved clinical question. The receiving office confirms receipt, but has not confirmed an appointment. Practice coordinator Mina is checking the status and will update Fin on Friday. Friday is a status-update commitment, not a clinical waiting instruction or appointment date. New or worsening concerns must use the practice's actual clinical route.

Dr Byrne: General practitioner / Fin: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has the specialist office confirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. Receipt of the referral

Only receipt is confirmed; the later stages have not been established.

2. Who is checking the status?

Answer and explanation

B. Mina

Mina is the practice coordinator assigned to check and update the status.

3. What does Friday mean?

Answer and explanation

D. A promised status update

Friday refers to communication about progress and is not a clinical waiting instruction.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Receipt, review, booking, attendance, and a returned clinical report are different stages. Do not report a later stage when only an earlier one is confirmed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
referral
Request for another professional or service to assess or assist.send a specialist referral
consultation question
Specific issue the referring clinician asks the receiving service to address.state the consultation question
urgency category
Assigned level of time sensitivity under a clinical process.verify the urgency category
referral receipt
Confirmation that the receiving service has obtained the referral.confirm referral receipt
appointment confirmation
Verification that a specific appointment has been arranged.obtain appointment confirmation
closed-loop referral
Referral process tracking receipt, follow-up, and information returned to the referrer.complete a closed-loop referral
consultant letter
Communication from a specialist summarizing an assessment or advice.review the consultant letter
awaiting review
Received but not yet evaluated by the relevant reviewer.mark a referral as awaiting review
missed appointment
Scheduled encounter that did not take place as arranged.follow up a missed appointment
results ownership
Responsibility for reviewing and acting on investigation results.clarify results ownership
pending action
Task still requiring completion.identify a pending action
acknowledgment
Confirmation that a message or request was received.obtain an acknowledgment
interim care
Care provided while another assessment or transition is pending.clarify interim care
contact preference
Person's preferred appropriate method of communication.verify the contact preference
escalation route
Process for raising an unresolved or urgent concern to the right professional.use the clinical escalation route
care transition
Movement of care between settings, professionals, or stages.support a care transition
record transfer
Appropriate movement of relevant information between care providers.verify record transfer
access barrier
Obstacle that makes obtaining a service difficult.identify an access barrier
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A status-update date must never imply that symptoms can safely wait until that date. Keep administrative progress and clinical escalation separate.

C Notice the language

Make the stage visible

Patients may understand referred to mean booked or accepted for treatment. Name the actual stage and retain responsibility for unresolved care rather than treating the referral as a transfer of every duty.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the confirmed stage
The receiving office has confirmed receipt.
Name the missing step
An appointment has not yet been confirmed.
Identify the owner
Mina is checking the status.
Define the date
Friday is our update date, not your appointment date.
Keep care connected
We need to clarify who handles the outstanding clinical questions.
Separate urgent concerns
New or worsening symptoms use the clinical route, not a routine status message.
Confirm contact
Which approved contact method should we use for the update?
Close the loop
We will track the response and the information returned after consultation.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Has confirmed versus will check
Separates a completed acknowledgment from a future task.
Not yet
Marks an unfinished stage without implying that it has been refused.
Update date versus appointment date
Prevents an administrative commitment from becoming a false booking promise.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which update is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Receipt is confirmed; an appointment is not.

Only receipt has been verified in the supplied referral history.

2. Which phrase could dangerously confuse two processes?

Answer and explanation

D. Wait until Friday with any new symptoms.

A routine administrative update cannot determine how long a new clinical concern can wait.

3. What makes the next step accountable?

Answer and explanation

D. A named owner and a defined update

Naming Mina and the update date makes the communication task explicit and trackable.

4. What remains important after sending a referral?

Answer and explanation

B. Following the response and clarifying continuing responsibilities

Sending a request is only one step in a connected referral process.

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Byrne: General practitioner / Fin: Patient

    2 is a separate step. Mina is checking the status with the receiving office.
  5. Fin
    What exactly is the specialist being asked to address? I do not want to arrive without understanding that.
  6. Dr Byrne
    We will review the 3 with you and make sure the relevant records accompany the request.
  7. Fin
    I can explain my concern, but I may not remember every detail that is already in your notes.
  8. Dr Byrne
    Appropriate 4 helps the receiving team. It does not replace listening to your current account.
  9. Fin
    Mina mentioned Friday. Is that when the consultation will happen, or when someone will contact me?
  10. Dr Byrne
    Friday is a status update. The 5 is checking progress, not attending a confirmed appointment.
  11. Fin
    Who should deal with questions before the specialist sees me? I do not want to fall between teams.
  12. Dr Byrne
    We must clarify 6 and the outstanding responsibilities rather than assume everything transferred when we sent the request.
  13. Fin
    If something changes clinically before Friday, should I wait for Mina's administrative update to mention it?
  14. Dr Byrne
    No. Use the actual clinical 7 for new or worsening concerns. The update date is not advice to wait.
  15. Fin
    I also need calls at a time I can answer privately. Can we agree on how the update reaches me?
  16. Dr Byrne
    Yes, we will verify your 8 and use the practice's approved communication process.
  17. Fin
    Once the appointment happens, will the specialist's advice come back here, or do I have to relay everything?
  18. Dr Byrne
    We will track the 9 and review the responsibilities it identifies, rather than assume attendance alone closes every question.
  19. Fin
    That is clearer: receipt now, a status update Friday, and no appointment date promised yet.
  20. Dr Byrne
    Exactly. A 10 keeps the stages and responsibilities connected until the relevant follow-up is complete.

Answers and explanations
  1. referral receipt Turn 2. Referral receipt confirms that the service obtained the request, not that an appointment has been arranged.
  2. Appointment confirmation Turn 4. Appointment confirmation establishes a specific booking and is a later stage than merely receiving the referral.
  3. consultation question Turn 6. The consultation question states the unresolved issue the referring clinician asks the specialist to address.
  4. record transfer Turn 8. Record transfer sends relevant information through the proper process so the receiving team has the necessary context.
  5. pending action Turn 10. A pending action is a task still to complete; here it is checking progress, not attending a booked consultation.
  6. interim care Turn 12. Interim care covers appropriate care while another assessment is pending and cannot be assumed to belong to nobody.
  7. escalation route Turn 14. The escalation route connects new or worsening clinical concerns to appropriate assessment, separate from routine referral tracking.
  8. contact preference Turn 16. Contact preference identifies an appropriate communication method and helps an update reach the patient without avoidable privacy problems.
  9. consultant letter Turn 18. A consultant letter communicates the specialist's assessment or advice back to the referring clinician for review.
  10. closed-loop referral Turn 20. A closed-loop referral tracks the request, response, and returned information instead of stopping at the act of sending.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Fin: I was told the referral had gone through. Does that mean the specialist has booked me in?
  2. Dr Byrne: We have referral receipt, but an appointment has not yet been confirmed.
  3. Fin: Thank you for separating those. I heard gone through and assumed there was a date somewhere.
  4. Dr Byrne: Appointment confirmation is a separate step. Mina is checking the status with the receiving office.
  5. Fin: What exactly is the specialist being asked to address? I do not want to arrive without understanding that.
  6. Dr Byrne: We will review the consultation question with you and make sure the relevant records accompany the request.
  7. Fin: I can explain my concern, but I may not remember every detail that is already in your notes.
  8. Dr Byrne: Appropriate record transfer helps the receiving team. It does not replace listening to your current account.
  9. Fin: Mina mentioned Friday. Is that when the consultation will happen, or when someone will contact me?
  10. Dr Byrne: Friday is a status update. The pending action is checking progress, not attending a confirmed appointment.
  11. Fin: Who should deal with questions before the specialist sees me? I do not want to fall between teams.
  12. Dr Byrne: We must clarify interim care and the outstanding responsibilities rather than assume everything transferred when we sent the request.
  13. Fin: If something changes clinically before Friday, should I wait for Mina's administrative update to mention it?
  14. Dr Byrne: No. Use the actual clinical escalation route for new or worsening concerns. The update date is not advice to wait.
  15. Fin: I also need calls at a time I can answer privately. Can we agree on how the update reaches me?
  16. Dr Byrne: Yes, we will verify your contact preference and use the practice's approved communication process.
  17. Fin: Once the appointment happens, will the specialist's advice come back here, or do I have to relay everything?
  18. Dr Byrne: We will track the consultant letter and review the responsibilities it identifies, rather than assume attendance alone closes every question.
  19. Fin: That is clearer: receipt now, a status update Friday, and no appointment date promised yet.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The referral went to the wrong service

A coordinator identifies a routing problem.

  1. Coordinator: The receiving service says this referral belongs elsewhere and has not accepted the patient.
  2. GP: We need to verify the correct route rather than mark the referral as completed.
  3. Coordinator: The original request was sent successfully, but that did not establish acceptance.
  4. GP: Exactly. Sent, received, accepted, and booked are different stages in the process.
  5. Coordinator: Who should review the clinical priority while the routing issue is resolved?
  6. GP: The responsible clinician must use the actual case, not infer urgency from administrative delay.
  7. Coordinator: I will keep the task open and record which service responded.
  8. GP: Please also identify who will contact the patient with the corrected arrangements.
  9. Coordinator: We should not leave the patient believing an appointment is already on its way.
  10. GP: Agreed. Accurate status and named responsibility matter as much as transmitting the document.
The patient has heard nothing

A patient asks about an unconfirmed referral.

  1. Patient: I was told a referral would be sent, but I have not heard about an appointment.
  2. GP: Let us check the actual status rather than assume that no news means it was accepted.
  3. Patient: I do not know whether it was sent, received, or reviewed by the service.
  4. GP: Those are separate stages, and we should explain which one is confirmed.
  5. Patient: Could you tell me who is responsible for checking with the receiving team?
  6. GP: We will identify that person and the actual follow-up route.
  7. Patient: I also need to know which clinical instructions apply while the appointment is unresolved.
  8. GP: Those must come from your actual care plan, not a guessed waiting period.
  9. Patient: Thank you. I want a clear next contact rather than another vague instruction to wait.
  10. GP: We will confirm the arrangement and keep any unresolved step visible.
A specialist response needs patient explanation

A clinician receives advice that has not reached the patient.

  1. Specialist: I have sent the reviewed recommendation, but I have not discussed it directly with the patient.
  2. GP: Thank you. We should not treat the letter's arrival as completed patient communication.
  3. Specialist: Please distinguish the recommendation from any decision that still needs an informed discussion.
  4. GP: I will review the actual advice and explain the relevant next step with the patient.
  5. Specialist: There is also a question that the available information did not resolve.
  6. GP: We will preserve that limitation rather than present the response as a complete answer.
  7. Specialist: Can you confirm who owns the follow-up after the explanation?
  8. GP: I will clarify responsibility under our actual clinical process and acknowledge any further referral.
  9. Specialist: Then the recommendation will not remain an unread attachment with an assumed outcome.
  10. GP: Correct. Receipt, interpretation, patient discussion, and agreed action each need their own accurate status.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Sent does not mean seen
  1. Read the corrected referral update. Stress receipt, appointment, and status update as separate stages.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the distinction between Friday's administrative update and the clinical escalation route.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Track a returned specialist report

A specialist consultation occurred Monday. The report has not arrived. Coordinator Zoe is requesting it and will update the referring team Wednesday. No new treatment instruction is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Clinician: "The consultation occurred on ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Monday

Monday is the completed attendance date stated in this follow-up case.

2. Zoe: "The report has not ___."

Answer and explanation

B. arrived

Attendance does not mean the consultant's report has reached the referring team.

3. Clinician: "The update is due ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Wednesday

Wednesday is the communication commitment, not a treatment deadline.

4. Zoe: "No new treatment instruction has been ___."

Answer and explanation

A. supplied

The exercise provides no new clinical instruction to infer or act on.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Professional audience: general practitioners, family physicians, primary-care doctors, family medicine residents

Lesson 08: Closing the referral loop
Original fictional case: Dr Byrne referred Fin to a specialist for an unresolved clinical question. The receiving office confirms receipt, but has not confirmed an appointment. Practice coordinator Mina is checking the status and will update Fin on Friday. Friday is a status-update commitment, not a clinical waiting instruction or appointment date. New or worsening concerns must use the practice's actual clinical route.
Language workshop: Sent does not mean seen
Communication goal: Give a referral update that identifies the open question, owner, and next contact.
Language guidance: Receipt, review, booking, attendance, and a returned clinical report are different stages. Do not report a later stage when only an earlier one is confirmed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The receiving office has confirmed receipt. / An appointment has not yet been confirmed. / Mina is checking the status. / Friday is our update date, not your appointment date. / We need to clarify who handles the outstanding clinical questions. / New or worsening symptoms use the clinical route, not a routine status message. / Which approved contact method should we use for the update? / We will track the response and the information returned after consultation. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: referral: Request for another professional or service to assess or assist. / consultation question: Specific issue the referring clinician asks the receiving service to address. / urgency category: Assigned level of time sensitivity under a clinical process. / referral receipt: Confirmation that the receiving service has obtained the referral. / appointment confirmation: Verification that a specific appointment has been arranged. / closed-loop referral: Referral process tracking receipt, follow-up, and information returned to the referrer. / consultant letter: Communication from a specialist summarizing an assessment or advice. / awaiting review: Received but not yet evaluated by the relevant reviewer. / missed appointment: Scheduled encounter that did not take place as arranged. / results ownership: Responsibility for reviewing and acting on investigation results. / pending action: Task still requiring completion. / acknowledgment: Confirmation that a message or request was received. / interim care: Care provided while another assessment or transition is pending. / contact preference: Person's preferred appropriate method of communication. / escalation route: Process for raising an unresolved or urgent concern to the right professional. / care transition: Movement of care between settings, professionals, or stages. / record transfer: Appropriate movement of relevant information between care providers. / access barrier: Obstacle that makes obtaining a service difficult. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected referral update. Stress receipt, appointment, and status update as separate stages. Swap roles. Repeat the distinction between Friday's administrative update and the clinical escalation route. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Byrne: General practitioner / Fin: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A specialist consultation occurred Monday. The report has not arrived. Coordinator Zoe is requesting it and will update the referring team Wednesday. No new treatment instruction is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Fin: Once the appointment happens, will the specialist's advice come back here, or do I have to relay everything?
Dr Byrne: We will track the consultant letter and review the responsibilities it identifies, rather than assume attendance alone closes every question.
Fin: That is clearer: receipt now, a status update Friday, and no appointment date promised yet.
Dr Byrne: Exactly. A closed-loop referral keeps the stages and responsibilities connected until the relevant follow-up is complete.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
